A game comonadic account of Courcelle and Feferman–Vaught–Mostowski theorems
Tomáš Jakl, Dan Marsden, Nihil Shah

Abstract
Game comonads, introduced by Abramsky, Dawar and Wang, and developed by Abramsky and Shah, give a categorical semantics for model comparison games. We present an axiomatic account of Feferman-Vaught-Mostowski (FVM) composition theorems within the game comonad framework, parameterized by the model comparison game. In a uniform way, we produce compositionality results for the logic in question, and its positive existential and counting quantifier variants. Secondly, we extend game comonads to the second order setting, specifically in the case of Monadic Second Order (MSO) logic. We then generalize our FVM theorems to the second order case. We conclude with an abstract formulation of Courcelle’s algorithmic meta-theorem, exploiting our earlier developments. This is instantiated to recover well-known bounded tree-width and bounded clique-width Courcelle theorems for MSO on graphs.

1
Introduction

Model comparison games, such as the Ehrenfeucht–Fraïssé
[
16
,
19
]
, pebbling
[
28
]
and bisimulation games
[
23
]
, play a key role in finite model theory
[
30
]
.
Recent work has introduced a novel categorical semantics for these games
[
1
,
4
]
. In this programme, a comonad is introduced for a chosen model comparison game. This comonad is typically graded by a resource parameter, such as quantifier depth or variable count. Winning Duplicator strategies for model comparison games, bounded by the chosen resource parameter, can then be uniformly characterized categorically, in terms of the Kleisli
[
25
]
and Eilenberg-Moore
[
17
]
categories of the comonad.

This approach has been extended to more complex games, such as those for guarded logics
[
2
]
and generalized quantifiers
[
9
]
, and applications such as Lovasz type theorems
[
14
]
and Rossman’s homomorphism theorem
[
38
]
. The abstract setting was axiomatized in
[
3
]
.

Our aim is to give a semantic account of algorithmic meta-theorems, specifically Courcelle type theorems
[
12
]
, exploiting the game comonadic semantics.
To do so, firstly we develop an axiomatic formulation of Feferman-Vaught-Mostowski (FVM) theorems
[
36
,
18
]
, which describe how logical equivalence is preserved under operations on models.

Pleasingly, the canonical categorical structures associated with the comonadic approach play a central role.
Specifically, we lift model transformations to the Kleisli and Eilenberg-Moore categories via distributive laws satisfying natural axioms. These axioms are semantic in nature, and parametric in the notion of model equivalence. Contrast this with the more tautological, MSO specific logical condition of MSOL-smoothness used to describe well-behaved model transformations for the generic Courcelle theorem presented in
[
32
]
. Our results yield FVM results for a given logic, it’s positive existential fragment, and its extension to counting quantifiers in a uniform way.

Secondly, we identify a suitable comonad capturing the model comparison game for monadic second order logic. FVM theorems often generalize to MSO
[
22
,
21
,
29
,
41
]
. Our abstract approach to FVM theorems is then extended to the second-order setting.

With these two components in place, we give an abstract Courcelle theorem, parameterized by the game comonad and model transformations of interest. This theorem is then instantiated to yield concrete results for bounded tree-width
[
10
]
and bounded clique-width variants
[
13
,
37
]
of Courcelle’s theorem.

2
Preliminaries

We assume the reader is already familiar with basic category theoretic notions such as functors, natural transformations, adjunctions, and limits (see e.g.
[
5
]
or
[
6
]
).

2.1
Game comonads

A
comonad
(in Kleisli form) on a category
\mathcal{C}
is a triple
(\mathbb{C},\varepsilon,(-)^{*})
where
\mathbb{C}
is an object map
\mathrm{obj}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathrm{obj}(\mathcal{C})
, the
counit

\varepsilon
is a
\mathrm{obj}({\mathcal{C}})
-indexed family of morphisms
\varepsilon_{A}\colon\mathbb{C}(A)\to A
. Lastly, there is a coextension operation
(-)^{*}
mapping morphisms
f:\mathbb{C}(A)\rightarrow B
to their coextension
f^{*}:\mathbb{C}(A)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}(B)
,
subject to the following equations:

\displaystyle(\varepsilon_{A})^{*}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{C}A},\quad\varepsilon_{B}\circ f^{*}=f,\quad(g\circ f^{*})^{*}=g^{*}\circ f^{*},

(1)

It is then standard
[
33
]
that
\mathbb{C}
is a functor such that
\mathbb{C}(f)=(f\circ\varepsilon_{A})^{*}
, and the counit is a natural transformation
\mathbb{C}\Rightarrow{Id}
. The morphisms
\delta_{A}:=\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{C}(A)}^{*}:\mathbb{C}(A)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{2}(A)
form a
comultiplication
natural transformation satisfying

\varepsilon\circ\delta=\mathrm{id}=\mathbb{C}(\varepsilon)\circ\delta
and
\delta\circ\delta=\mathbb{C}(\delta)\circ\delta
.

Our comonads of interest shall be on the category of relational structures over a fixed signature. A
signature

\sigma
is a set consisting of:

1.

Relation symbols
, each with a specified
arity
, a strictly positive natural number. A relation symbol of arity
n
is referred to as
n
-ary
.

\mathbf{rel}(\sigma)
shall denote the subset of relation symbols.

2.

Constant symbols
.
\mathbf{cs}(\sigma)
shall denote the subset of constant symbols.

A
modal signature

\sigma
has a single constant
c_{0}
and
\mathbf{rel}(\sigma)
consisting of a single binary relation
E
, and finitely many unary relations.

A
\sigma
-structure
A
consists of a set
A
, referred to as the
universe
. For each
R\in\mathbf{rel}(\sigma)
of arity
n
, an
n
-ary relation
R^{A}
on
A
, and for each
c\in\mathbf{cs}(\sigma)
an element
c^{A}\in A
. A
morphism of
\sigma
-structures

f:A\rightarrow B
is a function of type
A\rightarrow B
, preserving relations and constants. That is, for
n
-ary
R\in\mathbf{rel}(\sigma)
,
R^{A}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\Rightarrow R^{B}(f(a_{1}),\ldots,f(a_{n}))
, and for
c\in\mathbf{cs}(\sigma)
,
f(c^{A})=c^{B}
.

\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
shall denote the category of
\sigma
-structures and morphisms between them.

We shall require three comonads on
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
for the examples in later sections, parameterized by a positive integer
k
. These are small generalizations of the key comonads appearing in
[
4
]
, to handle signatures with constants in a uniform way.

We shall write
A^{+}
for the set of non-empty lists over
A
, and
A^{\leq k}
for its restriction to lists of at most
k
elements.

Comonad
\mathbb{E}_{k}
:

The universe of
\mathbb{E}_{k}(A)
is
A^{\leq k}\uplus\mathbf{cs}(\sigma)
. For
c\in\mathbf{cs}(\sigma)
, define
c^{\mathbb{E}_{k}(A)}:=c
. The counit preserves constants, and on lists
\varepsilon[a_{1},\dots,a_{n}]:=a_{n}
.
For
n
-ary
R\in\mathbf{rel}(\sigma)
,
R^{\mathbb{E}_{k}(A)}(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})
iff the
s_{i}\not\in\mathbf{cs}(\sigma)
are pairwise comparable in the prefix order, and

R^{A}(\varepsilon(s_{1}),\ldots,\varepsilon(s_{n}))
.
For
h:\mathbb{E}_{k}(A)\rightarrow B
, the coextension preserves constants, and acts on lists as

h^{*}([a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}]):=[h([a_{1}]),\ldots,h([a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}])]
.

Comonad
\mathbb{M}_{k}
:

We restrict to a modal signature. The universe of
\mathbb{M}_{k}(A)
consists of
\{c_{0}\}
and the elements of
A^{\leq k}
of the form
[a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}]
such that
E^{A}(c_{0}^{A},a_{1})
and for
1\leq i<n
,
E^{A}(a_{i},a_{i+1})
. The counit and coextension act as for
\mathbb{E}_{k}
.
We have
E^{\mathbb{M}_{k}(A)}(s,s^{\prime})
iff either
s=c_{0}
and
s^{\prime}
is a one element list, or
s
and
s^{\prime}
are lists with
s^{\prime}
covering
s
in the prefix order. For unary
P\in\mathbf{rel}(\sigma)
,
P^{\mathbb{M}_{k}(A)}(s)
iff
P^{A}(\varepsilon(s))
.

Comonad
\mathbb{P}_{k}
:

The universe of
\mathbb{P}_{k}(A)
is
(\{0,\ldots,k-1\}\times A)^{+}\,\uplus\,\mathbf{cs}(\sigma)
. Constant interpretations are defined as for
\mathbb{E}_{k}
. The counit
\varepsilon
preserves constants, and on lists
sends
[(p_{1},a_{1}),\dots,(p_{n},a_{n})]
to
a_{n}
.
We refer to the first element in a pair
(p,a)
as a
pebble index
. For
n
-ary
R\in\mathbf{rel}(\sigma)
,

R^{\mathbb{P}_{k}(A)}(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})
iff:

1.

The
s_{i}\not\in\mathbf{cs}(\sigma)
are pairwise comparable in the prefix order.

2.

For
s_{i},s_{j}\not\in\mathbf{cs}(\sigma)
with
s_{i}\leq s_{j}
, the pebble index of the tail of
s_{i}
does not appear in the suffix of
s_{i}
in
s_{j}
.

3.

R^{A}(\varepsilon(s_{1}),\ldots,\varepsilon(s_{n}))
.

For
h\colon\mathbb{P}_{k}(A)\rightarrow B
, the coextension
h^{*}
preserves constants, and acts on lists as:

h^{*}([(p_{1},a_{1}),\ldots,(p_{n},a_{n})]):=[(p_{1},h([a_{1}])),\ldots,(p_{n},h([a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}]))]

Theorem 1
.

\mathbb{E}_{k}
,
\mathbb{M}_{k}
and
\mathbb{P}_{k}
are comonads in Kleisli form (direct adaptation of
[
1
,
4
]
).

2.2
Kleisli and Eilenberg–Moore categories

A comonad
\mathbb{C}
on a category
\mathcal{C}
gives rise to two categories. The first is the
Kleisli category

\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{C})
consisting of the same objects as
\mathcal{C}
has, but morphisms from
A
to
B
are
\mathcal{C}
-morphisms of type
\mathbb{C}(A)\to B
. The composition of
f\colon\mathbb{C}A\to B
and
g\colon\mathbb{C}B\to C
is defined as
g\circ f^{*}
, with the counits the identity morphisms. The equations (
1
) ensure that
\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{C})
is a well-formed category.

The second category induced by
\mathbb{C}
is the
Eilenberg–Moore category
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
of
\mathbb{C}
-coalgebras
. Its objects are pairs
(A,\alpha)
where
\alpha\colon A\to\mathbb{C}(A)
is a
\mathcal{C}
-morphism such that

\varepsilon_{A}\circ\alpha=\mathrm{id}_{A}\kern 5.0pt\text{and}\kern 5.0pt\delta\circ\alpha=\mathbb{C}\alpha\circ\alpha,

(2)

A
coalgebra morphism

h\colon(A,\alpha)\to(B,\beta)
is a
\mathcal{C}
-morphism
h\colon A\to B
such that
\beta\circ h=\mathbb{C}h\circ\alpha
. We shall simply write
\alpha
instead of
(A,\alpha)
whenever
A
can be inferred from the context.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\mathbb{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathbb{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{\mathbb{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{\mathbb{C}}}

\dashv

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\mathbb{C}}}

\dashv

The two categories induce two adjunctions over the base category and a functor
K^{\mathbb{C}}
from
\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{C})
to
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
, as depicted on the right.
For our purposes it suffices to know that

•

K^{\mathbb{C}}
is a fully faithful functor such that
K^{\mathbb{C}}\circ F_{\mathbb{C}}=F^{\mathbb{C}}
.

•

F_{\mathbb{C}}
is the identity on objects, and for
\mathcal{C}
-morphism
f\colon A\to B
,
F_{\mathbb{C}}(f)=f\circ\varepsilon_{A}
.

•

U^{\mathbb{C}}
is the forgetful functor, sending
(A,\alpha)
to
A
and
F^{\mathbb{C}}
is the
cofree coalgebra
functor, sending
A
in
\mathcal{C}
to
(\mathbb{C}A,\delta_{A})
and
f\colon A\to B
in
\mathcal{C}
to
\mathbb{C}f
.

Remark 2
.

A coalgebra
(A,\alpha)
of any of the game comonads defined above is endowed with a preorder
\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}
, where
x\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}y
whenever the word
\alpha(x)
is a prefix of
\alpha(y)
.
Then, by (
2
),
\alpha(y)
, for a non-constant
y
, is the word listing all non-constant
x
such that
x\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}y
.

In fact, the category
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{E}_{k})
is equivalent to the category of
\sigma
-structures endowed with a compatible forest order of depth
{\leq}\,k
, see section 9 in
[
4
]
for details.

2.3
Factorisation systems

Strong homomorphisms
in
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
are those homomorphisms
f\colon A\to B
that reflect relations, i.e. if
(f(x_{1}),\dots,f(x_{n}))\in R^{B}
then
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in R^{A}
.
Recall that any homomorphism
h\colon A\to B
in
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
factors into an onto homomorphism
q\colon A\twoheadrightarrow h[A]
followed by a strong injective homomorphism
m\colon h[A]\rightarrowtail B
.
Then,
h[A]
above is the
\sigma
-structure on the image
h[A]
of
h
so that the inclusion
h[A]\to B
is a strong homomorphism. This factorisation is a special case of a general categorical structure which we review next.

A
factorisation system

(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
consists of a class of morphisms
\mathcal{E}
called
quotients
, denoted by
\twoheadrightarrow
, and a class of morphisms
\mathcal{M}
called
embeddings
, denoted by
\rightarrowtail
, which are both closed under compositions, contain all isomorphisms and, furthermore,

•

every morphism
f\colon A\to B
has a factorisation
f=m\circ e
for some quotient
e\colon A\twoheadrightarrow C
and embedding
m\colon C\rightarrowtail B
, and

•

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

every
e\in\mathcal{E}
is
orthogonal
to every
m\in\mathcal{M}
, i.e. given
u,v
making the diagram on the right commute,
there exists a unique morphism
d
, making the two triangles commute.

If, furthermore,
\mathcal{E}
is a class of epimorphisms and
\mathcal{M}
is a class of monomorphisms, we say that
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
is a
proper factorisation system
.

We make use of the following well-known fact that any comonad
\mathbb{C}
which
restricts to embeddings
, i.e. sends
\mathcal{M}
-morphisms to
\mathcal{M}
-morphisms, lifts
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
to the factorisation system
(\overline{\mathcal{E}},\overline{\mathcal{M}})
on
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
, where
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
consists of all morphisms of coalgebras
h
such that
U(h)
is in
\mathcal{E}
and, similarly,
\overline{\mathcal{M}}
consists of morphisms
h
such that
U(h)
is in
\mathcal{M}
.

Lemma 3
.

(\overline{\mathcal{E}},\overline{\mathcal{M}})
is a factorisation system and it is proper whenever
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
is.

In the following we always assume that categories of relational structures
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
come equipped with the (surjective, strong injective) factorisation system
1
1

1

Categorically, surjective homomorphisms are epis and strong injective homomorphisms are regular monos in
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
.
. It is immediate to see that
\mathbb{E}_{k}
preserves strong injective homomorphisms and, hence, by the previous lemma, this factorisation system lifts to the categories of
\mathbb{E}_{k}
-coalgebras. Moreover, the same is true for pebbling and modal comonads and their categories of coalgebras.

2.4
Game comonads and logical equivalences

An important feature of game comonads is that they semantically characterise logical equivalences with respect to various fragments and extensions of first-order logic. Let
\text{FO}_{k}
be the fragment of first-order logic over signature
\sigma
restricted to sentences of quantifier rank
k
, i.e. when the depth of nesting of quantifiers is at most
k
. Then,
\exists^{+}_{k}
is the further refinement of
\text{FO}_{k}
to positive existential sentences, i.e. sentences without negation or universal quantification, and
\#\text{FO}_{k}
is the extension of
\text{FO}_{k}
by allowing counting quantifiers. These are quantifiers
\exists_{\geq n}
where
A\models\exists_{\geq n}x.\varphi(x)
if there are at least
n
different
a\in A
such that
A\models\varphi(a)
.

As we will see, the Ehrenfeucht–Fraïssé comonad
\mathbb{E}_{k}
characterises logical equivalence with respect to all three aforementioned fragments. To this end, define

•

A\leftrightarrows_{\mathbb{C}}B
if there exist morphisms
\mathbb{C}(A)\to B
and
\mathbb{C}(B)\to A
, and

•

A\cong_{\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{C})}B
if
F_{\mathbb{C}}(A)\cong F_{\mathbb{C}}(B)
in
\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{C})
.

In fact, these equivalences are all statements about free coalgebras as
A\cong_{\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{C})}B
is equivalent to
F^{\mathbb{C}}(A)\cong F^{\mathbb{C}}(B)
in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
, since
K^{\mathbb{C}}
is fully faithful, and similarly,
A\leftrightarrows_{\mathbb{C}}B
iff some
F^{\mathbb{C}}(A)\to F^{\mathbb{C}}(B)
and
F^{\mathbb{C}}(A)\to F^{\mathbb{C}}(B)
exist.

Following
[
1
]
, to capture logics with equality we introduce an extended signature
\sigma^{I}
with a fresh binary relation
I
, and a
translation functor

\mathfrak{t}^{I}\colon\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma)\to\mathcal{R}(\sigma^{I})

sending
A\in\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma)
to
(A,I^{A})
, where
(a,b)\in I^{A}
iff
a=b
. Here,
\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma)
denotes the discrete subcategory of
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
containing only identity morphisms. Since
\mathbb{E}_{k}
was defined for an arbitrary relational signature, there is an instance of
\mathbb{E}_{k}
over
\mathcal{R}(\sigma^{I})
, which we don’t distinguished notationally.

Proposition 4

(by adapting
[
1
, theorems 13 and 16]
)
.

For
\sigma
-relational structures
A,B
,

•

A\equiv_{\exists^{+}_{k}}B
iff
A\leftrightarrows_{\mathbb{E}_{k}}B
iff
\mathfrak{t}^{I}A\leftrightarrows_{\mathbb{E}_{k}}\mathfrak{t}^{I}B

•

A\equiv_{\#\text{FO}_{k}}B
iff
\mathfrak{t}^{I}A\cong_{\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{E}_{k})}\mathfrak{t}^{I}B

Capturing
\equiv_{\text{FO}_{k}}
requires a few more definitions. First, the category of
paths

\mathcal{P}
is the full subcategory of
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{E}_{k})
consisting of the coalgebras
(A,\alpha)
such that the order
\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}
(cf. remark
2
) is a finite linear order. Recall from section
2.3
that the factorisation system (surjective, strong injective) on
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
lifts to the category of
\mathbb{E}_{k}
-coalgebras.
We call a morphism
h\colon(A,\alpha)\to(B,\beta)
in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{E}_{k})
a
pathwise-embedding
, if for any path embedding

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(P,\pi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Q,\rho)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A,\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B,\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

e\colon(P,\pi)\rightarrowtail(A,\alpha)
(with
(P,\pi)\in\mathcal{P}
),
the composite
h\circ e
is also an embedding.
Further, we say that
h
is
open
if every commutative square as shown on the right,
where
(P,\pi)
and
(Q,\rho)
are from
\mathcal{P}
, has a diagonal filler
d\colon(Q,\rho)\to(A,\alpha)
making the two triangles commute. Finally, define

•

A\leftrightarrow_{\mathbb{C}}B
if there exists a span of open pathwise-embeddings
F^{\mathbb{C}}(A)\leftarrow R\rightarrow F^{\mathbb{C}}(B)
.

Proposition 5

(by adapting
[
4
, theorem 10.5]
)
.

A\equiv_{\text{FO}_{k}}B
iff
\mathfrak{t}^{I}A\leftrightarrow_{\mathbb{E}_{k}}\mathfrak{t}^{I}B
.

The pebbling comonad
\mathbb{P}_{k}
captures the corresponding
k
-variable fragments (as opposed to quantifier rank fragment) in the same way and, similarly,
\mathbb{M}_{k}
captures the fragments of modal logic restricted to formulas of modal depth
{\leq}\,k
.

Remark 6
.

We always assume that the subcategory of paths is given implicitly.
In particular, for any category
\mathcal{C}
equipped with a factorisation system
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
, we take the full subcategory of paths
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
to be the objects whose class of non-isomorphic subobjects (w.r.t
\mathcal{M}
-morphisms) forms a finite chain under the order induced by
\mathcal{M}
-morphisms. In particular, for the game comonads we discuss, the subcategory of
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
is equivalent to subcategory of the coalgebras
(A,\alpha)
where the order
\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}
is a finite linear order
[
3
]
.

3
Comonadic FVM Theorems

3.1
A Motivating FVM-type Example

In the approach to algorithmic meta-theorems such as Courcelle’s theorem adopted in later sections,
we work with classes of structures built up by a suitable family of operations. These operations must interact well
with logical equivalence in the sense of Feferman–Vaught–Mostowski.
We now outline a simple motivating example, forming coproducts of structures,
and the relationship to first order logic. We use the Ehrenfeucht–Fraïssé comonad, highlighting the key themes, and emphasizing connections to game comonads. The finer technical details are developed in later sections.

Fix a relational signature
\sigma
without constants. For two structures
A
and
B
, their coproduct
A+B
is simply
the disjoint union of the two components, with the induced relational structure.
We would like to show that, if
A_{1}\equiv_{\text{FO}_{k}}B_{1}
and
A_{2}\equiv_{\text{FO}_{k}}B_{2}
, then also
A_{1}+A_{2}\equiv_{\text{FO}_{k}}B_{1}+B_{2}
. Equivalently, in view of proposition
5
, given spans of open pathwise-embeddings

F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A_{i}))\xleftarrow{f_{i}}R_{i}\xrightarrow{g_{i}}F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(\mathfrak{t}^{I}(B_{i}))
for
i\in\{1,2\}
,
we would like to construct a span of open pathwise embeddings of the form

\displaystyle F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A_{1}+A_{2}))\leftarrow R\rightarrow F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(\mathfrak{t}^{I}(B_{1}+B_{2})).

(3)

Observe that, even though the category of coalgebras has coproducts for general reasons, a span of the form in (
3
) cannot be formed using the coproduct of the two component spans. We would obtain a span of type

F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A_{1}))+F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A_{2}))\xleftarrow{f_{1}+f_{2}}R_{1}+R_{2}\xrightarrow{g_{1}+g_{2}}F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(\mathfrak{t}^{I}(B_{1}))+F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(\mathfrak{t}^{I}(B_{2}))

In general
F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A_{1}))+F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A_{2}))
and
F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A_{1}+A_{2}))
need not be isomorphic, so our span is of the wrong type. Conceptually, this approach fails because the span we require should encode a strategy for play that may switch back and forth between two components, and forming coproducts keeps all the data entirely independent.

We observe that if we have a natural transformation

\kappa\colon\mathbb{E}_{k}(A+B)\to\mathbb{E}_{k}(A)+\mathbb{E}_{k}(B)

that satisfies some natural equations
with respect to the counit and comultiplication, then this
induces a lifted functor
\mathbin{\overline{+}}:\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{E}_{k})\times\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{E}_{k})\rightarrow\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{E}_{k})
such that
F_{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(A+B)=F_{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(A)\mathbin{\overline{+}}F_{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(B)
.
As
F_{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(A)=A
, the existence of such a natural transformation yields both:

\displaystyle A_{1}\equiv_{\exists^{+}_{k}}A_{2}\;\mbox{ and }\;B_{1}\equiv_{\exists^{+}_{k}}B_{2}\;

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\;A_{1}+B_{1}\equiv_{\exists^{+}_{k}}A_{2}+B_{2}

\displaystyle A_{1}\equiv_{\#\text{FO}_{k}}A_{2}\;\mbox{ and }\;B_{1}\equiv_{\#\text{FO}_{k}}B_{2}\;

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\;A_{1}+B_{1}\equiv_{\#\text{FO}_{k}}A_{2}+B_{2}

So we have FVM type results for the positive existential fragment, and
FO
with counting quantifiers. For full first-order logic, we require an additional observation. As
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{E}_{k})
has equalizers, we can lift coproducts to a functor
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{E}_{k})\times\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{E}_{k})\rightarrow\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{E}_{k})
by forming the equalizer:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\mathbin{\widehat{+}}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(A+B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(\mathbb{E}_{k}(A)+\mathbb{E}_{k}(B))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(\kappa)\circ\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(\alpha+\beta)}

Crucially, this functor satisfies
F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(A+B)\cong F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(A)\mathbin{\widehat{+}}F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(B)
. We can then verify the following by direct calculation.

Lemma 7
.

Given a pair of open pathwise-embeddings
f,g
in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{E}_{k})
, the morphism
f\mathbin{\widehat{+}}g
is also an open pathwise-embedding.

We see that the following span

F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A_{1}))\mathbin{\widehat{+}}F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A_{2}))\xleftarrow{f_{1}\mathbin{\widehat{+}}f_{2}}R_{1}\mathbin{\widehat{+}}R_{2}\xrightarrow{g_{1}\mathbin{\widehat{+}}g_{2}}F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(\mathfrak{t}^{I}(B_{1}))\mathbin{\widehat{+}}F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(\mathfrak{t}^{I}(B_{2}))

is a span of open pathwise-embeddings, by lemma
7
. Moreover,
F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A_{1}))\mathbin{\widehat{+}}F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A_{2}))
is isomorphic to
F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A_{1})+\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A_{2}))
and
similarly for
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
,
and
\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A)+\mathfrak{t}^{I}(B)\cong\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A+B)
,
yielding a span of shape (
3
), and so
A_{1}+A_{2}\equiv_{\text{FO}_{k}}B_{1}+B_{2}
.

Remark 8
.

The above analysis follows from a well-known theory of commutative (resp. lax monoidal) monads
[
26
]
. They lift the monoidal structure to the Kleisli and Eilenberg-Moore categories (if sufficiently complete
[
15
,
27
]
). The liftings of coproducts in this section is arising from the dual of these results, as
\mathbb{E}_{k}
is opmonoidal with respect to coproducts.

Motivated by this example, in the sequel we introduce a general framework for lifting logical equivalences. The general results deal with operations of arbitrary finite arity, with unary and binary transformations being of primary interest in applications. We will also need to deal with varying base categories and comonads, as signatures, resource parameters or even logics may vary under model transformations. As was the case in this example, this leads to FVM theorems for a given logic, its positive existential fragment, and its extension with counting quantifiers, parametric in the game comonad of interest. The main technical challenge is giving sufficient conditions for the analogue of the critical lemma
7
to hold.

3.2
FVM-Theorems from Kleisli-laws

As we saw in section
3.1
, in order to develop FVM-type theorems within the comonadic framework, it is natural to consider functorial transformations of models.
We consider categories
\mathcal{C}_{1}
, …,
\mathcal{C}_{n}
and
\mathcal{D}
, equipped with comonads
\mathbb{C}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{C}_{n}
, and
\mathbb{D}
, respectively, and a functorial operation
H\colon\prod_{i}\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\mathcal{D}
.

Remark 9
.

To simplify our notation for objects and morphisms in product categories, we shall write

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle A_{i}\hfil

and

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle f_{i}\hfil

instead of the longer expressions
A_{1},\dots,A_{n}
and
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}
, respectively.

A
Kleisli-law
is a natural transformation
\kappa\colon\mathbb{D}\circ H\to H\circ\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}_{i}

such that:

(F1)

H(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\varepsilon_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\varepsilon_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\varepsilon_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\varepsilon_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}})\circ\kappa=\varepsilon_{H}

(F2)

H(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\delta_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}})\circ\kappa=\kappa\circ\mathbb{D}\kappa\circ\delta_{H}

These laws are direct generalisations of Kleisli-laws lifting of unary functor to the Kleisli category, see e.g.
[
34
]
. The following result is probably folklore
[
24
]
:

Proposition 10
.

There is a bijective correspondence between:

1.

Kleisli-laws of type

\mathbb{D}\circ H\to H\circ\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}_{i}
.

2.

Functors
\overline{H}:\prod_{i}\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{C}_{i})\rightarrow\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{D})
such that:

\overline{H}\circ\prod_{i}F_{\mathbb{C}_{i}}=F_{\mathbb{D}}\circ H
.

It is encouraging for the game comonad approach that this classical theory immediately yields results for logical equivalences.
To this end, let
\approx_{\mathbb{C}_{i}}
and
\approx_{\mathbb{D}}
be binary relations on
\mathcal{C}_{i}
and
\mathcal{D}
objects respective. We shall say that
H
preserves
\approx
if for all
1\leq i\leq n
,
A_{i}\mathbin{\approx_{\mathbb{C}_{i}}}B_{i}
, then

H(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})\mathbin{\approx_{\mathbb{D}}}H(B_{1},\ldots,B_{n})
.

Theorem 11
.

If
\kappa:\mathbb{D}\circ H\rightarrow H\circ\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}_{i}
t is a Kleisli-law then
H
preserves both
\leftrightarrows
and
\cong_{\mathsf{Kl}}
.

Example 12

(Comonad Morphisms)
.

A
comonad morphism

\sigma:\mathbb{C}\Rightarrow\mathbb{D}
is a natural transformation such that

\varepsilon\circ\sigma=\varepsilon
and
\delta\circ\sigma=\mathbb{D}(\sigma)\circ\sigma\circ\delta
.
A comonad morphism is a special case of a Kleisli-law, of type
\mathbb{C}\circ{Id}\Rightarrow{Id}\circ\mathbb{D}
, corresponding to liftings of the identity functor.
For example, there is a comonad morphism of type
\mathbb{M}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}_{2}
, with action preserving constants, and mapping lists to lists pebbled by the parity of their position:

[a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\ldots,a_{n}]\mapsto[(1,a_{1}),(0,a_{2}),(1,a_{3})\ldots,(p_{n},a_{n})]

where
p_{n}=n\mod 2
.
Consequently, utilizing the logical characterizations analogous to proposition
4
in the cases of (full, unbounded depth)
\mathbb{M}
and
\mathbb{P}_{2}

[
1
,
4
]
, we obtain a semantic translation of positive
\square
-free modal logic into positive existential two variable logic and graded modal logic into two variable logic with counting quantifiers.

3.3
Lifting operations to categories of coalgebras

Assuming appropriate equalisers in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})
exist, we can extend the above lifting to the categories of coalgebras
\widehat{H}\colon\prod_{i}\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C}_{i})\rightarrow\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})
. We define
\widehat{H}
pointwise as the equaliser, for coalgebras
(A_{1},\alpha_{1})\in\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C}_{1})
, …,
(A_{n},\alpha_{n})\in\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C}_{n})
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern (4)

In fact, the two parallel morphisms are a
reflexive pair
. That is, they have a mutual post-inverse.

Proposition 13
.

If
\kappa
is a Kleisli-law, and
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})
has equalizers of reflexive pairs, then
\widehat{H}
is a well-defined functor such that
F_{\mathbb{D}}\circ H\cong\widehat{H}\circ\prod_{i}F^{\mathbb{C}_{i}}
.

Note that existence of said equalisers is guaranteed for our game comonads.

Lemma 14
.

For a comonad
\mathbb{C}
over
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
. If
\mathbb{C}
preserves embeddings then the category
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
has equalisers.

The same is true for a comonad
\mathbb{C}
over any wide
2
2

2

Recall that a subcategory is
wide
if it contains the same objects, with only morphisms restricted.
subcategory
\mathcal{C}
of
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
with coproducts, all embeddings of
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
and satisfying that if
f\circ g\in\mathcal{C}
, for some
f,g\in\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
, then also
g\in\mathcal{C}
.

3.4
Smooth Kleisli laws

In this section we observe that for an operation to preserve
\leftrightarrow
we require the lifted operation to preserve open pathwise-embeddings. Since open pathwise-embeddings are defined in terms of embeddings and paths, we require that the categories
\mathcal{C}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{n+1}
3
3

3

It is notationally convenient to denote the codomain category and comonad
\mathcal{C}_{n+1}
and
\mathbb{C}_{n+1}
, rather than
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathbb{D}
is this section. As before, we assume that
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C}_{n+1})
has equalisers of reflexive pairs.

are equipped with proper factorisation systems and the comonads preserve embeddings (cf. lemma
3
).
We then say an operation
H\colon\prod\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\mathcal{C}_{n+1}
with a Kleisli law
\kappa
is
smooth
4
4

4

Mirroring the terminology introduced in
[
32
]
.
if
\widehat{H}(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})
is an open pathwise-embedding for every tuple of open pathwise-embeddings
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}
. Smoothness immediately guarantees FVM-type theorem for back-and-forth equivalence.

Proposition 15
.

If
H
admits a smooth Kleisli law, then
H
preserves
\leftrightarrow
.

Recall that
\equiv_{\text{FO}_{k}}
is obtained as the
\leftrightarrow_{\mathbb{E}_{k}}
relation preceded by the
\mathfrak{t}^{I}
translation. The following is one of our main definitions in this paper, tailored to account for this translation.

Definition 16
.

We say that an ‘operation’ functor
H\colon\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma_{i})\to\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma_{n+1})
is
(\mathbb{C}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{C}_{n+1})
-smooth relative to
\mathfrak{t}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{t}_{n+1}
, where
\mathfrak{t}_{i}\colon\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma_{i})\to\mathcal{C}_{i}
is a functor and
\mathbb{C}_{i}
a comonad on
\mathcal{C}_{i}
, for
1\leq i\leq n+1
, if there exists a functor
H^{\mathfrak{t}}\colon\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\mathcal{C}_{n+1}
and natural transformations

\theta\colon H^{\mathfrak{t}}\circ\prod\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}\mathfrak{t}_{i}\to\mathfrak{t}_{n+1}\circ H\quad\text{and}\quad\kappa\colon\mathbb{C}_{n+1}\circ H^{\mathfrak{t}}\to H^{\mathfrak{t}}\circ\prod\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{C}_{i}

such that
\kappa
is a smooth Kleisli law and
F^{\mathbb{C}_{n+1}}(\theta)
is a collection of open pathwise embeddings.

Lastly, we say that
H
is
FO-
(\mathbb{C}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{C}_{n+1})
-smooth
if it is
(\mathbb{C}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{C}_{n+1})
-smooth relative to
\mathfrak{t}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{t}_{n+1}
where each
\mathfrak{t}_{i}\colon\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma_{i})\to\mathcal{C}_{i}
is a codomain restriction of the usual FO translation functor
\mathfrak{t}^{I}\colon\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma_{i})\to\mathcal{R}^{S}(\sigma^{I}_{i})
.

By convention, call an
n
-ary operation
\mathbb{C}
-smooth if it is
(\mathbb{C},\dots,\mathbb{C})
-smooth, for the same game comonad
\mathbb{C}
but possibly
n
different instances for
n
different signatures.

Corollary 17
.

FO-
\mathbb{E}_{k}
-smooth operations preserve
\equiv_{\text{FO}_{k}}
.

Similar statements also hold for
\mathbb{P}_{k}
,
\mathbb{M}_{k}
and their corresponding logical equivalences.

Example 18
.

In the case of first-order equivalence, and the coproduct operation, definition
16
is instantiated with every comonad
\mathbb{C}_{i}
as
\mathbb{E}_{k}
, and
\mathfrak{t}_{i}=\mathfrak{t}^{I}
. The natural transformation
\kappa
is as mentioned in section
3.1
and
\theta
is the isomorphism
\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A)+\mathfrak{t}^{I}(B)\cong\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A+B)
. Therefore, coproducts are FO-
\mathbb{E}_{k}
-smooth and preserves
\equiv_{\text{FO}_{k}}
.

3.5
Practical axioms for smoothness

The notion of smoothness in definition
16
is extremely general, but the required conditions can be cumbersome to verify.
We now identify sufficient conditions for a Kleisli-law to be smooth, that are easier to establish in practice. We introduce the following axioms:

(S1)

H\colon\prod_{i}\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\mathcal{D}
restricts to embeddings.

0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle g_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern
