0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\rho_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\rho_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\rho_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\rho_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}})\to\widehat{H}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\alpha_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\alpha_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\alpha_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\alpha_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}})
,
for some path embeddings
g_{i}\colon\rho_{i}\rightarrowtail\alpha_{i}
, there exist (necessarily unique) morphisms
h_{i}\colon\pi_{i}\to\rho_{i}
such that
e_{i}=g_{i}\circ h_{i}
, for
i=1,\dots,n
.

A necessary step before showing sufficiency of
(S1)
and
(S2)
, is checking the following.

Proposition 19
.

If
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}
are embeddings (or pathwise-embeddings) in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C}_{1})
, …,
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C}_{n})
, respectively, then
\widehat{H}(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})
is an embedding (or pathwise-embedding).

The main difficulty is in verifying that openness for pathwise-embeddings is also preserved, which is the case.

Theorem 20
.

A Kleisli law which satisfies
(S1)
and
(S2)
is smooth.

3.6
Example: edge creation

We apply our comonadic analysis to the investigation of bounded clique-width structures in section
5.2
.
An operation important in the construction of bounded clique-width structures is edge-creation
{\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R}}
, for some
r
-ary
R\in\sigma
, where
\sigma
is without constants.
The operation
{\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R}}
, as all the operations for forming a
\sigma
-structure of clique-width
\leq m
, apply to structures in an expanded signature
5
5

5

In fact, this is slightly more general. Clique-width is typically defined for graphs rather than relational structures.

\sigma_{m}=\sigma\cup\{P_{1},\dots,P_{m}\}
where
P_{i}
are unary relations, or ‘colours’. The operation
{\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R}}
takes a list of colours
i=(i_{1},\dots,i_{r})
with
i_{j}\in\{1,\dots,m\}
and adds all tuples in
P_{i_{1}}\times\dots\times P_{i_{r}}
to the interpretation of
R
.

It is clear that
{\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R}}\colon\mathcal{R}(\sigma_{m})\to\mathcal{R}(\sigma_{m})
is a functor. Furthermore, we have a natural transformation

\kappa\colon\mathbb{E}_{k}\circ{\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R}}\to{\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R}}\circ\mathbb{E}_{k}

with components
\kappa_{A}
that send
w\in\mathbb{E}_{k}({\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R}}(A))
to the same word
w
in
{\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R}}(\mathbb{E}_{k}A)
which satisfies

(F1)
,
(F2)
,
(S1)
and
(S2)
.

Lemma 21
.

\kappa\colon\mathbb{E}_{k}\circ{\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R}}\to{\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R}}\circ\mathbb{E}_{k}
is a smooth Kleisli law.

For any signature
\sigma
,
{\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R}}
does not change the interpretation of any relation in
\sigma\backslash\{R\}
, so we obtain a natural isomorphism
\theta:{\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R}}(\mathfrak{t}^{I})\cong\mathfrak{t}^{I}({\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R}})
with components given by the identity as a set function. Therefore,
{\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R}}
is FO-
\mathbb{E}_{k}
-smooth.

3.7
Example: parallel sums

Maybe the most prominent example of a FVM theorem is the one for coproducts. Here we verify smoothness for a generalisation of coproducts. Let
\sigma
be a relational signature without constants and let
\sigma_{1}
,
\sigma_{2}
and
\sigma_{3}
be extensions of
\sigma
with constants such that
\mathbf{cs}(\sigma_{1})\cup\mathbf{cs}(\sigma_{2})=\mathbf{cs}(\sigma_{3})
.
This operation is slightly more subtle than the previous examples. In order for our Kleisli law to be natural, we need to restrict the homomorphisms in our categories of interest. Therefore, we work with the restriction
\mathcal{R}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma)
of
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
to the morphisms which send non-constant elements to non-constant elements. Define a binary operation

\mathbin{\parallel}\colon\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma_{1})\times\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma_{2})\to\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma_{3})

sending
A\in\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma_{1})
and
B\in\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma_{2})
to the quotient
(A+B)/_{\approx}
where
A+B
is the
\sigma
-structure coproduct and
\approx
the least equivalence relation identifying pairs
(c^{A},c^{B})
such that
c\in\mathbf{cs}(\sigma_{1})\cap\mathbf{cs}(\sigma_{2})
. For a constant
c\in\mathbf{cs}(\sigma_{1})
we set
c^{A\mathbin{\parallel}B}=\mathfrak{i}(c^{A})
and, similarly, for
c\in\mathbf{cs}(\sigma_{2})
set
c^{A\mathbin{\parallel}B}=\mathfrak{i}(c^{B})
, where
\mathfrak{i}
is the quotient map
A+B\to A\mathbin{\parallel}B
. Observe that
\mathfrak{i}(A)\cap\mathfrak{i}(B)=\{c^{A\mathbin{\parallel}B}\mid c\in\mathbf{cs}(\sigma_{1})\cap\mathbf{cs}(\sigma_{2})\}
.

The operation
\mathbin{\parallel}
extends to a functor
\mathcal{R}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma_{1})\times\mathcal{R}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma_{2})\to\mathcal{R}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma_{3})
. Furthermore, it admits a smooth Kleisli law w.r.t. a comonad
\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}
on
\mathcal{R}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma_{i})
(for
i=1,2,
or
3
), which is the restriction of
\mathbb{E}_{k}
to words that do not contain any constant letter. Then, the Kleisli law is the following mapping, for every
A\in\mathcal{R}(\sigma_{1})
and
B\in\mathcal{R}(\sigma_{2})
, defined on non-constants by

\displaystyle\kappa\colon\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}(A\mathbin{\parallel}B)\longrightarrow\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}(A)\mathbin{\parallel}\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}(B),

\displaystyle w\in{(A\mathbin{\parallel}B)}^{\leq k}

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}\mathtt{ex}_{1}(w)&\text{if }\varepsilon(w)\in\mathfrak{i}(A)\\
\mathtt{ex}_{2}(w)&\text{if }\varepsilon(w)\in\mathfrak{i}(B)\\[5.0pt]
\end{cases}

where
\mathtt{ex}_{1}([x_{1},\dots,x_{n}])
is the word
6
6

6

We use the Haskell-style list comprehension notation, for a word
w=[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]
the word
[x_{i}\mid\varphi(i)]
is the restriction of
w
to the letters on positions
i
such that
\varphi(i)
holds.

[x_{i}\mid x_{i}\in\mathfrak{i}(A)]
and, similarly,
\mathtt{ex}_{2}([x_{1},\dots,x_{n}])
is
[x_{i}\mid x_{i}\in\mathfrak{i}(B)]
. One can verify that
\kappa
satisfies axioms
(S1)
and
(S2)
, which gives us:

Lemma 22
.

\kappa
is a smooth Kleisli law
(\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}\circ\mathbin{\parallel})\rightarrow(\mathbin{\parallel}\circ(\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}\times\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}))
.

In fact,
\mathbin{\parallel}
is FO-
\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}
-smooth as
\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A)\mathbin{\parallel}^{I}\mathfrak{t}^{I}(B)\cong\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A\mathbin{\parallel}B)
, where
\mathbin{\parallel}^{I}
is the
\sigma^{I}
variant of
\mathbin{\parallel}
. Further, it also follows that
\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A)\leftrightarrow_{\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}}\mathfrak{t}^{I}(B)
iff
A\equiv_{\text{FO}_{k}}B
, giving us the following.

Proposition 23
.

If
A_{i}\equiv_{\text{FO}_{k}}B_{i}
in
\mathcal{R}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma_{i})
, for
i=1,2
, then
A_{1}\mathbin{\parallel}A_{2}\,\equiv_{\text{FO}_{k}}\,B_{1}\mathbin{\parallel}B_{2}
.

3.8
Other examples

Example 24

(Products for
FO
)
.

For any comonad, there is a canonical Kleisli law for products, and when the Eilenberg-Moore category has equalizers, the lifted functor yields products.
By observing that pairs are equal if and only if each component is equal, we obtain an isomorphism
\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A\times B)\cong\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A)\times\mathfrak{t}^{I}(B)
.
We can confirm that products of coalgebras preserve open pathwise embeddings,
and therefore products are
FO
-
\mathbb{E}_{k}
-smooth.

Example 25

(Comonad Morphisms)
.

Example
12
exhibited a comonad morphism
\kappa:\mathbb{M}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}_{2}
yielding a semantic translation of positive
\square
-free modal logic into two-variable positive existential logic and graded modal logic into two-varable logic with counting quantifiers. The intermediate translation from full modal logic into two-variable logic is witnessed by
\kappa
being
(\mathbb{M},\mathbb{P}_{2})
-smooth.

4
Comonads for MSO

4.1
\mathbb{E}_{k}
for many-sorted structures

In order to extend our use of
\mathbb{E}_{k}
to
MSO
we interpret the syntax of
MSO
as a two-sorted first-order logic.
We then use a translation functor to fix the semantics of
MSO
to coincide with the standard semantics.
This requires us to ‘upgrade’ the
\mathbb{E}_{k}
comonad to handle two-sorted structures. For simplicity and generality, we extend our definition of
\mathbb{E}_{k}
to accommodate all many-sorted structures for fixed set
S
of sorts.

A signature
\sigma
on sorts
S
contains a set of constant symbols
\mathbf{cs}(\sigma,s)
for each
s\in S
and a set of relation symbols
R
with an associated arity given by a tuple of sorts
{\langle s_{1},\dots,s_{r}\rangle\in S^{r}}
.
An object
A
in the category of
\sigma
-structures
\mathcal{R}^{S}(\sigma)
has a universe given by an
S
-indexed family of sets
\{A_{s}\}_{s\in S}
along with interpretations
c^{A}\in A_{s}
for every constant symbol
c\in\mathbf{cs}(\sigma,s)
and
R^{A}\subseteq A_{s_{1}}\times\dots\times A_{s_{r}}
for every relation symbol with arity
\langle s_{1},\dots,s_{r}\rangle
in
\sigma
.
A morphism
f:A\rightarrow B
in
\mathcal{R}^{S}(\sigma)
is a
S
-indexed family of functions
\{f_{s}:A_{s}\rightarrow B_{s}\}
which preserves all constants and all relations.

These objects provide the semantics for
S
-sorted first-order logic.
Both syntax and semantics of
S
-sorted first order logic
\text{FO}(S)
are similar to the single-sorted case. The key difference is that terms (constants and variables) always have an associated sort.
Consequently, the quantifiers
\exists x{:}s
,
\forall x{:}s
range over the set
A_{s}
when interpreted in the model
A
.
For subset
J\subseteq S
,
\text{FO}_{k}(S,J)
denotes
S
-sorted first order logic up to quantifier rank
k
with equality for terms with sorts
s\in J
.

We can now describe
\mathbb{E}_{k}
as a comonad over
\mathcal{R}^{S}(\sigma)
.
The universe of
\mathbb{E}_{k}A
for object
A
in
\mathcal{R}^{S}(\sigma)
has components
(\mathbb{E}_{k}A)_{s}
equal to the disjoint union of
\mathbf{cs}(\sigma,s)
and the set of words of the form
[a_{1}{:}s_{1},\dots,a_{n}{:}s_{n}]
where
n\leq k
and, for all
i\leq n
,
s_{i}\in S
,
a_{i}\in A_{s_{i}}
and
s_{n}=s
.
The counit
\varepsilon_{A}
for
A\in\mathcal{R}^{S}(\sigma)
and coextension
f^{*}:\mathbb{E}_{k}A\rightarrow\mathbb{E}_{k}B
for
f:\mathbb{E}_{k}A\rightarrow B
are defined as in the single-sorted case while respecting the sorts.
We can perform a similar upgrade to the single-sorted
\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}
comonad to obtain a many-sorted
\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}
comonad.

Proposition 26
.

\mathbb{E}_{k}
and
\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}
are comonads over
\mathcal{R}^{S}(\sigma)
and
\mathcal{R}^{S}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma)
, respectively.

Just as with the single-sorted case, to capture equality for the sorts
J\subseteq S
, we consider an extended signature
\sigma^{J}
which has additional relations
I_{s}
of arity
\langle s,s\rangle
for every
s\in J
. Let
\mathfrak{t}^{J}\colon\mathcal{R}^{S}_{0}(\sigma)\rightarrow\mathcal{R}^{S}(\sigma^{J})
be the functor that sends
A
to the same structure where the additional relations are interpreted as
(a,b)\in I^{A}_{s}
iff
a=b\in A_{s}
for
s\in J
.
Let
\mathfrak{t}^{J}_{\text{nc}}\colon\mathcal{R}^{S}_{0}(\sigma)\rightarrow\mathcal{R}^{S}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma^{J})
be the codomain restriction of
\mathfrak{t}^{J}
.
We then show that a version of proposition
5
still holds for these many-sorted
\mathbb{E}_{k}
and
\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}
comonads.

Proposition 27
.

Given
A,B\in\mathcal{R}^{S}_{0}(\sigma)
,

•

A\equiv_{\text{FO}_{k}(S,J)}B
iff
\mathfrak{t}^{J}A\leftrightarrow_{\mathbb{E}_{k}}\mathfrak{t}^{J}B
.

•

A\equiv_{\text{FO}_{k}(S,J)}B
iff
\mathfrak{t}^{J}_{\text{nc}}A\leftrightarrow_{\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}}\mathfrak{t}^{J}_{\text{nc}}B
, if
s\in J
for every sort
s\in S
such that
\mathbf{cs}(\sigma,s)\not=\emptyset
.

4.2
The two-sorted translation for
MSO

Second-order logic can be treated syntactically as a many-sorted first-order logic (see e.g.
[
42
,
35
]
).
In particular, we consider
\text{MSO}_{k}
on signature
\sigma
as
\text{FO}_{k}(S,J)
with
S=\{\mathbf{f},\mathbf{m}\}
and
J=\{\mathbf{f}\}
on an expanded signature
\sigma^{e}
where
\mathbf{f}
is sort for ordinary
f
irst-order terms, and
\mathbf{m}
is a sort for
m
onadic second-order set variables. The expanded
S
-sorted signature
\sigma^{e}
has constants
\mathbf{cs}(\sigma^{e},\mathbf{f})=\mathbf{cs}(\sigma)
and
\mathbf{cs}(\sigma^{e},\mathbf{m})=\varnothing
; and relations
R
of arity
\langle\mathbf{f},\dots,\mathbf{f}\rangle
(
\mathbf{f}
repeated
r
times) for every
R\in\sigma
of arity
r
, and also
e
of arity
\langle\mathbf{f},\mathbf{m}\rangle
and
I
of arity
\langle\mathbf{f},\mathbf{f}\rangle
.

The additional expressive power of
MSO
over
\text{FO}(S,J)
is obtained by restricting the semantics of
MSO
to only include the class of ‘standard’ models of
MSO
rather than all
\sigma^{e}
-structures.
We pick out these standard models using a functor
\mathfrak{t}^{e}\colon\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma)\rightarrow\mathcal{R}^{S}(\sigma^{e})
where, for a
\sigma
-structure
A
, the two sorts are instantiated as
\mathfrak{t}^{e}(A)_{\mathbf{f}}=A
and
\mathfrak{t}^{e}(A)_{\mathbf{m}}=\mathcal{P}(A)
, the constants
c^{\mathfrak{t}^{e}A}=c^{A}
and relations

R^{\mathfrak{t}^{e}(A)}=R^{A},\text{ for }R\in\sigma,\qquad e^{\mathfrak{t}^{e}(A)}(a,M)\kern 5.0pt\text{iff}\kern 5.0pta\in M\qquad I^{\mathfrak{t}^{e}(A)}(a,a^{\prime})\kern 5.0pt\text{iff}\kern 5.0pta=a^{\prime}

Let
\mathfrak{t}^{e}_{\text{nc}}\colon\mathcal{R}^{S}_{0}(\sigma^{e})\rightarrow\mathcal{R}^{S}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma^{e})
be the codomain restriction of
\mathfrak{t}^{e}
.
We can adapt the syntactic translation stated in
[
42
]
to translate
\text{MSO}_{k}
sentences in signature
\sigma
(i.e.
MSO
sentences of quantifier rank
{\leq}\,k
) to
\text{FO}_{k}(S,J)
sentences in
\sigma^{e}
.
Linking this syntactic translation to the semantic translation
\mathfrak{t}^{e}
and utilizing proposition
27
, we are able to capture
\equiv_{\text{MSO}_{k}}
using the many-sorted
\mathbb{E}_{k}
and
\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}
:

Theorem 28
.

A\equiv_{\text{MSO}_{k}}B
iff
\mathfrak{t}^{e}A\leftrightarrow_{\mathbb{E}_{k}}\mathfrak{t}^{e}B
iff
\mathfrak{t}^{e}A\leftrightarrow_{\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}}\mathfrak{t}^{e}B

The last equivalence follows from the second item of proposition
27
as the condition is satisfied by
J=\{\mathbf{f}\}
and
\mathbf{cs}(\sigma^{e},\mathbf{f})=\mathbf{cs}(\sigma)
. Next we also adapt definition
16
to this setting.

Definition 29
.

We say that
H
is
MSO
-
(\mathbb{C}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{C}_{n+1})
-smooth
if it is
(\mathbb{C}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{C}_{n+1})
-smooth relative to
\mathfrak{t}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{t}_{n+1}
where each
\mathfrak{t}_{i}\colon\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma_{i})\to\mathcal{C}_{i}
is a codomain restriction of the MSO translation functor
\mathfrak{t}^{e}\colon\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma_{i})\to\mathcal{R}^{S}(\sigma^{e}_{i})
.

As before, proposition
15
and theorem
28
imply that the MSO-
\mathbb{E}_{k}
-smooth (or MSO-
\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}
-smooth) functors preserve
\equiv_{\text{MSO}_{k}}
.

4.3
Example: edge creation

In this section, we demonstrate the
{\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R}}
operation is
MSO
-
\mathbb{E}_{k}
-smooth. Since this operation is used in generating structures of bounded clique-width, this is a step towards recovering Courcelle’s theorem for bounded clique-width structures using our comonadic framework.

By definition the functor
{\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R}}\colon\mathcal{R}(\sigma_{m})\rightarrow\mathcal{R}(\sigma_{m})
only changes the interpretation of
R\in\sigma_{m}
.
In the two-sorted setting of
\sigma^{e}
, we define a variant of this functor
{\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R,e}}
of type
\mathcal{R}^{S}(\sigma^{e}_{m})\rightarrow\mathcal{R}^{S}(\sigma^{e}_{m})
which only changes interpretation of the corresponding
R\in\sigma^{e}_{m}
with arity
\langle\mathbf{f},\dots,\mathbf{f}\rangle
. This ensures that there is an isomorphism
\theta:{\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R,e}}(\mathfrak{t}^{e}(A))\cong\mathfrak{t}^{e}({\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R}}(A))
, for any
\sigma_{m}
-structure
A
. In addition to the existence of
\theta
, in order for
{\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R}}
to be
MSO
-
\mathbb{E}_{k}
-smooth, we need a smooth Kleisli law

\kappa\colon\mathbb{E}_{k}\circ{\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R,e}}\rightarrow{\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R,e}}\circ\mathbb{E}_{k}.

The components of
\kappa_{A}
are given by
S
-sorted functions where
\kappa_{A,t}
sends a word
w\in(\mathbb{E}_{k}({\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R,e}}(A)))_{t}
to the same word
w\in({\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R,e}}(\mathbb{E}_{k}(A)))_{t}
for
t\in S=\{\mathbf{f},\mathbf{m}\}
.
The proof that this
\kappa
satisfies
(S1)
and
(S2)
is a direct adaptation of the single-sorted proof (cf. section
3.6
).

Lemma 30
.

For every
r
-ary relation
R\in\sigma
and
r
-tuple of labels

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle i\hfil

, the operation
{\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R}}
is
MSO
-
\mathbb{E}_{k}
-smooth.

4.4
Example: twisted sums

Here we show that
\mathbin{\parallel}\colon\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma_{1})\times\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma_{2})\to\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma_{3})
, defined in section
3.7
, is
MSO
-
\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}
-smooth.
To this end, we define a “twisted sum” functor

\bowtie\colon\mathcal{R}^{S}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma_{1}^{e})\times\mathcal{R}^{S}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma_{2}^{e})\to\mathcal{R}^{S}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma_{3}^{e})

as the lift of
\mathbin{\parallel}
along
\mathfrak{t}^{e}
. For
A\in\mathcal{R}^{S}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma_{1}^{e})
and
B\in\mathcal{R}^{S}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma_{2}^{e})
, the
\sigma^{I}_{3}
-structure reduct of
A\bowtie B
is precisely
A^{\prime}\mathbin{\parallel}B^{\prime}
where
A^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime}
are the
\sigma^{I}_{1}
– and
\sigma^{I}_{2}
-reducts of
A
and
B
, respectively. As before, we have the map
\mathfrak{i}\colon A_{\mathbf{f}}+B_{\mathbf{f}}\to(A_{\mathbf{f}}+B_{\mathbf{f}})/_{\approx}
. What remains to define is the second sort
(A\bowtie B)_{\mathbf{m}}
, which is just
A_{\mathbf{m}}\times B_{\mathbf{m}}
, and the relation
e^{A\bowtie B}
, which is the set
\{(\mathfrak{i}(a),(a^{\prime},b^{\prime}))\mid e^{A}(a,a^{\prime}),\,b^{\prime}\in B_{\mathbf{m}}\}\cup\{(\mathfrak{i}(b),(a^{\prime},b^{\prime}))\mid a^{\prime}\in A_{\mathbf{m}},\,e^{B}(b,b^{\prime})\}
.

We now define the natural transformation
\theta\colon\mathfrak{t}^{e}(A)\bowtie\mathfrak{t}^{e}(B)\to\mathfrak{t}^{e}(A\mathbin{\parallel}B)
. In the
\mathbf{f}
-sort it is just the identity function and in the
\mathbf{m}
-sort we send the pair
(M,N)
of subsets
M\subseteq A
and
N\subseteq B
to the subset
\mathfrak{i}(M)\cup\mathfrak{i}(N)
of
A\mathbin{\parallel}B
. Clearly,
\theta
is a strong onto homomorphism in
\mathcal{R}^{S}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma^{e}_{3})
and, therefore,
F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}}(\theta)
is an open pathwise embedding.

Lastly, we need a smooth Kleisli law
\kappa\colon\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}(A\bowtie B)\to\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}(A)\bowtie\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}(B)
. Analogously to section
3.7
, define
\mathtt{ex}_{1}
by sending a word
w=[x_{1}:s_{1},\dots,x_{n}:s_{n}]
from
\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}(A\bowtie B)
to
\mathtt{ex}_{1}(w)=[y_{i}:s_{i}\mid x_{i}\in\mathfrak{i}(A_{\mathbf{f}})\text{ or }s_{i}=\mathbf{m}]
, where
y_{i}=a
if
x_{i}=\mathfrak{i}(a)
or
y_{i}=a^{\prime}
if
s_{i}=\mathbf{m}
and
x_{i}=(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})
. Then,
\mathtt{ex}_{2}
is defined dually. We define
\kappa(w)
, for a non-constant
w
, as
\mathtt{ex}_{1}(w)
if
\varepsilon(w)\in\mathfrak{i}(A_{\mathbf{f}})
,
\mathtt{ex}_{2}(w)
if
\varepsilon(w)\in\mathfrak{i}(B_{\mathbf{f}})
and as
(\mathtt{ex}_{1}(w),\mathtt{ex}_{2}(w))
for
w
from the
\mathbf{m}
-sort.

Proposition 31
.

\kappa
is a smooth Kleisli law and
\mathbin{\parallel}
is
MSO
-
\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}
-smooth.

4.5
Extensions of
MSO

The generality of definition
16
and the many-sorted
\mathbb{E}_{k}
allows us to adapt our approach to proving FVM and consequently Courcelle theorems for fragments and extensions of (monadic) second order logic. We can accomplish this by choosing the appropriate translation functors
\mathfrak{t}
. For example, the logic
\text{MSO}2
, which allows quantification over subsets of tuples in relations, could be captured by having a sort
\mathbf{s}_{R}
for each relation
R\in\sigma
and the translation functor
\mathfrak{t}
on a structure
A
sets
\mathfrak{t}(A)_{\mathbf{s}_{R}}
to
\mathcal{P}(R^{A})
. Similar translations can be tailored for weak MSO, counting MSO, and
n
-adic second-order logic.

Remark 32

(Failure of FVM theorems)
.

Unlike the case of
FO
in example
24
, there famously fails to be a FVM product theorem for
MSO
(see e.g.
[
32
]
). In our framework, the failure of
\times
to be
MSO
-
\mathbb{E}_{k}
-smooth is demonstrated by the non-existence of a natural transformation
\theta:\mathfrak{t}^{e}(A)\times\mathfrak{t}^{e}(B)\rightarrow\mathfrak{t}^{e}(A\times B)
where the components of
F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(\theta)
are open pathwise embeddings. Similarly, the non-existence of a suitable
\theta
or smooth Kleisli law
\kappa
could be used to demonstrate the impossibility of a FVM product theorem for
\text{MSO}2
and a FVM coproduct theorem for
n
-adic second-order logic with
n\geq 2
.

5
Comonadic Courcelle’s theorems

In the following we state an abstract version of Courcelle’s theorem, for classes of
\tau
-structures given by a
\tau
-presentation
\left<\mathcal{B},\mathcal{H}\right>
over
\Sigma
. This is specified by

•

a finite set of signatures
\Sigma
, which are extensions of
\tau
,

•

a finite set of generators
\mathcal{B}
, where each
B\in\mathcal{B}
is an object of
\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma)
, for some
\sigma\in\Sigma
, and

•

a finite set of operations
\mathcal{H}
, where each
H\in\Sigma
is a functor
\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma_{i})\to\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma)
, with
\sigma\in\Sigma
and
\sigma_{i}\in\Sigma
, for every
i
.

Intuitively,
\left<\mathcal{B},\mathcal{H}\right>
is a presentation of the class
\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{0}(\tau)
where
A\in\Gamma
if and only if there is a term
t_{A}
using objects
B\in\mathcal{B}
as constants and functors
H\in\mathcal{H}
as operations, such that the
\tau
-structure reduct of
t_{A}
is isomorphic to
A
.
Examples of classes with such presentations are the classes of structures of tree-width
{\leq}\,k
or clique-width
{\leq}\,k
. These are the focus of our attention in the following sections, once we state our comonadic Courcelle theorem.

To this end, we say that a comonad
\mathbb{C}
over
\mathcal{B}
has
finite
\mathfrak{t}
-type
, for a functor
\mathfrak{t}\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
, if the equivalence relation
\leftrightarrow_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathfrak{t}}
has finitely many equivalence classes, where
A\leftrightarrow_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathfrak{t}}B
iff
\mathfrak{t}A\leftrightarrow_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{t}B
.
A standard tree-automata argument yields the following.

Theorem 33
.

Let
\Delta
be a class of
\tau
-structures and let
\left<\mathcal{B},\mathcal{H}\right>
be a
\tau
-presentation over
\sigma_{1}
, …,
\sigma_{n}
and, for
1\leq i\leq n
let
\mathfrak{t}_{i}\colon\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma_{i})\to\mathcal{C}_{i}
be a functor and
\mathbb{C}_{i}
a comonad over
\mathcal{C}_{i}
. Assume further that

1.

\mathcal{C}_{i}
is a category with a proper factorisation system,

2.

\mathbb{C}_{i}
has finite
\mathfrak{t}_{i}
-type, preserves embeddings and
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C}_{i})
has equalisers of reflexive pairs,

3.

operations in
\mathcal{H}
are
(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\mathbb{C}_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\mathbb{C}_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\mathbb{C}_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{C}_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}})
-smooth relative to

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle\mathfrak{t}_{i}\hfil

, and

4.

for every
1\leq i\leq n
, the class of
\sigma_{i}
-structures with their
\tau
-structure reduct in
\Delta
is closed under
\leftrightarrow_{\mathbb{C}_{i}}^{\mathfrak{t}_{i}}
.

Then there exists an algorithm, which for a given
\tau
-structure
A
given as a term
t_{A}
of
\left<\mathcal{B},\mathcal{H}\right>
, decides
A\in\Delta
in linear time, in the size of
t_{A}
.

This theorem directly applies to our setting of
MSO
-
\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}
-smooth operations. Indeed, the (surjective, strong injective) factorisation system of
\mathcal{R}^{S}(\sigma^{e})
restricts to
\mathcal{R}^{S}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma^{e})
.
Further, observe that
\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}
preserves embeddings, has finite
\mathfrak{t}^{e}_{\text{nc}}
-type, because there are only finitely many
\equiv_{\text{MSO}_{k}}
-equivalence classes (cf. proposition
27
), and the category
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}})
has equalisers (cf. lemma
14
).
An
MSO
sentence
\varphi
of quantifier rank
{\leq}\,k
, in signature
\tau
, defines the class
\Delta_{\varphi}
of
\tau
-structures such that
A\in\Delta_{\varphi}
iff
A\models\alpha
. Observe that
\Delta_{\varphi}
automatically satisfies item 4 in theorem
33
.
Indeed,
\varphi
is a valid
MSO
sentence in any signature
\sigma
extending
\tau
. Consequently, if
A\leftrightarrow_{\mathbb{E}_{k}}^{e}B
and the
\tau
-structure reduct of
A
is in
\Delta_{\varphi}
then so is
B
.
Therefore:

Corollary 34
.

Let
\left<\mathcal{B},\mathcal{H}\right>
be a
\tau
-presentation and
\varphi
be a
MSO
sentence in signature
\tau
of quantifier rank
{\leq}\,k
.

If every
H\in\mathcal{H}
is
MSO
-
\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}
-smooth then there is an algorithm deciding
A\models\varphi
, for an arbitrary
A
given as a term
t_{A}
of
\left<\mathcal{B},\mathcal{H}\right>
, running in linear time, in the size of
t_{A}
.

5.1
Courcelle’s tree-width theorem

Tree-width is an important, well-understood graph parameter, popularised by the work of Robertson–Seymour on graph minors
[
39
]
. It is well-known
[
11
,
12
]
that a graph or, more generally a
\sigma
-structure, has tree-width
{<}\,m
if and only if it belongs to the class presented by
\left<\mathcal{B}_{tw},\mathcal{H}_{tw}\right>
over
\Sigma_{tw}
where

•

\Sigma_{tw}=\{\sigma_{C}\mid C\subseteq\{c_{1},\dots,c_{m}\}\}
where
\sigma_{C}
the extension of
\sigma
by constants in
C
,

•

\mathcal{B}_{tw}
consists of all
\sigma_{C}
-structures on
{\leq}\,m
vertices, with
C\subseteq\{c_{1},\dots,c_{m}\}
, and

•

\mathcal{H}_{tw}
consists of all parallel sums
\mathbin{\parallel}
of type

\mathcal{R}(\sigma_{C})\times\mathcal{R}(\sigma_{D})\to\mathcal{R}(\sigma_{C\cup D})
, for every
C,D
, and operations
\operatorname{fg}_{C,D}\colon\mathcal{R}(\sigma_{C})\to\mathcal{R}(\sigma_{D})
, for
D\subseteq C
, where
\operatorname{fg}_{C,D}(A)
is the
\sigma_{D}
-structure reduct of the
\sigma_{C}
-structure
A
.

In proposition
31
we have established that the parallel sum operations in
\mathcal{H}
are
MSO
-
\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}
-smooth. Furthermore, checking smoothness for
\operatorname{fg}_{C,D}
is also straightforward.
Consequently, corollary
34
for
\left<\mathcal{B}_{tw},\mathcal{H}_{tw}\right>
entails the classical Courcelle’s theorem.

Theorem 35
.

Let
\varphi
be a
MSO
sentence in signature
\tau
of quantifier rank
{\leq}\,k
. Then, there exists an algorithm deciding
A\models\varphi
, for an arbitrary
A
given by a term
t_{A}
of
\left<\mathcal{B}_{tw},\mathcal{H}_{tw}\right>
, running in linear time, in the size of
t_{A}
.

Remark 36
.

In traditional presentations of Courcelle’s theorem, the algorithm first computes the term
t_{A}
, given a structure
A
of tree-width
\leq k
. This part of the algorithm also runs in linear time in the size of
A

[
7
]
.

5.2
Courcelle’s clique-width theorem

In order to recover Courcelle’s theorem for structures of bounded clique-width as a corollary of theorem
33
, we note that the class of structures of clique-width
\leq m
is given by the
\sigma
-presentation
\left<\mathcal{B}_{cw},\mathcal{H}_{cw}\right>
over
\Sigma_{cw}
where

•

\Sigma_{cw}=\{\sigma_{m}\}
, recall that
\sigma_{m}=\sigma\cup\{P_{1},\dots,P_{m}\}
with each
P_{i}(\cdot)
unary

•

\mathcal{B}_{cw}
is the set of
\sigma_{m}
-strutures
\{v_{1},\dots,v_{m}\}
where
v_{i}
is the singleton
\{*\}
such that
P^{v_{i}}_{i}=\{*\}
and
R^{v_{i}}=\varnothing
for all
R\in\sigma_{m}\setminus\{P_{i}\}

•

\mathcal{H}_{cw}
consists of coproducts
\oplus
of type
\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma_{m})\times\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma_{m})\to\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma_{m})
, edge-creation operations
{\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R}}
for all
R\in\sigma
of arity
r
with
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle i\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle i\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle i\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle i\hfil$\crcr}}}\in\{1,\dots,m\}^{r}
, and relabeling operations
\operatorname{relab}_{i,j}
for all
i,j\in\{1,\dots,m\}
. The relabeling operation
\operatorname{relab}_{i,j}
‘recolours’ elements of colour
i
with the colour
j
:

P^{\operatorname{relab}_{i,j}(A)}_{j}=P^{A}_{i}\cup P^{A}_{j}\qquad P^{\operatorname{relab}_{i,j}(A)}_{i}=\varnothing\qquad R^{\operatorname{relab}_{i,j}(A)}=R^{A}\text{, for }R\in\sigma_{m}\setminus\{P_{i},P_{j}\}

The coproduct
\oplus
of type
\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma_{m})\times\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma_{m})\to\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma_{m})
is a special case of the
MSO
-
\mathbb{E}_{k}
-smooth operation
\mathbin{\parallel}
mentioned in proposition
31
. In lemma
30
we established all the edge-creation operations
{\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R}}
are
MSO
-
\mathbb{E}_{k}
-smooth. Verifying that the relabeling operations
\operatorname{relab}_{i,j}
are
MSO
-
\mathbb{E}_{k}
-smooth is straightforward.
Consequently, corollary
34
for
\left<\mathcal{B}_{cw},\mathcal{H}_{cw}\right>
entails Courcelle’s theorem for bounded clique-width structures.

Theorem 37
.

Let
\varphi
be a
MSO
sentence in signature
\tau
of quantifier rank
{\leq}\,k
. Then, there exists an algorithm deciding
A\models\varphi
, for an arbitrary
A
given by a term
t_{A}
of
\left<\mathcal{B}_{cw},\mathcal{H}_{cw}\right>
, running in linear time, in the size of
t_{A}
.

6
Conclusion

We presented a general semantic framework for FVM-type theorems, based on distributive laws. By varying the choice of game comonads, our results are parametric in the logic of interest, and yield FVM-type results for the logic. Moreover, we proved several new FVM-type results for positive existential and counting quantifier variants of these logics by applying theorem
11
to the relevant game comonad. We combined this with a game comonadic
semantics for monadic second order model equivalence. Both were illustrated by many examples, including FVM-type results spanning these different logics, and detailed expositions of the key operations for constructing graphs of bounded tree-width and clique-width. Combining our accounts of FVM-type theorems and monadic second order logic, we presented an abstract Courcelle theorem. This theorem was then instantiated to recover concrete tree-width and clique-width Courcelle theorems.

For reasons of exposition we have concentrated on relational structures. In fact theorem
33
, and other results, can be stated much more generally. It would be interesting to explore algorithmic meta-theorems in such broader settings, for example
[
8
]
.

The original Feferman-Vaught theorem
[
18
]
deals with operations on indexed families of structures, with the indexing itself carrying structure. We have not needed this level of generality in the present work, but the extension to account for this wider class of operations would be a natural next step.

Appendix A
Omitted Proofs

In the following we make use of the following elementary properties of proper factorisations systems.

Lemma A.1

(e.g. Section 2 in
[
20
]
)
.

Given a proper factorisation system
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
,

1.

\mathcal{E}\cap\mathcal{M}=\mathrm{Iso}
,

2.

if
g\circ f\in\mathcal{E}
then
g\in\mathcal{E}
and, dually,

if
g\circ f\in\mathcal{M}
then
f\in\mathcal{M}
,

3.

\mathcal{E}
contains all coequalisers and
\mathcal{M}
all equalisers.

We confirm the following generalization to signatures with constants of results in
[
4
]
.
See
1

Proof.

In the following
\mathbb{N}
denotes the set of natural numbers,
not
a comonad.
Fix a signature
\sigma
, potentially containing constant symbols.
In
[
1
,
4
]
a pebbling comonad
\mathbb{P}_{k}
for signatures without constants is defined. In the proofs, the bound on the number of pebbles plays no essential role, so we
can equally define a comonad
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{N}}
on
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{rel}(\sigma))
, with a pebble for each natural number.

The universe of
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{N}}(A)
is
(\mathbb{N}\times A)^{+}
with
p\in\mathbb{N}
. The counit extracts the second element of the tail of its input list.
For
R\in\mathbf{rel}(\sigma)
of arity
n
,
R^{\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{N}}(A)}(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})
if:

1.

The
s_{i}
are pairwise comparable in the prefix order.

2.

If
s_{i}<s_{j}
, then the pebble index of the tail of
s_{i}
does not appear in the suffix of
s_{i}
in
s_{j}
.

3.

R^{A}(\varepsilon(s_{1}),\ldots,\varepsilon(s_{n}))

The coextension of
h:\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{N}}(A)\rightarrow B
is given by:

\displaystyle h^{*}(

\displaystyle[(p_{0},a_{0}),\ldots,(p_{n},a_{n})]):=

\displaystyle[(p_{0},[(p_{0},a_{0})]),\ldots,(p_{n},[(p_{0},a_{0}),\ldots,(p_{n},a_{n})])]

Assuming
\mathbf{cs}(\sigma)
has
m
elements, with a specified linear ordering
c_{0},\ldots,c_{m-1}
, we can restrict
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{N}}
to a comonad
\mathbb{P}_{m+\mathbb{N}}
, on
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{rel}(\sigma))
, by restricting to lists:

1.

With length greater than
m
.

2.

With the
m
element prefix of each list being

[(0,c^{A}_{0}),\ldots,(m-1,c^{A}_{m-1})]

3.

The pebble indices in positions
m
and above are greater than or equal to
m
.

We then observe that this yields a comonad on
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
if we define for
0\leq i<m
:

c^{\mathbb{P}_{m+\mathbb{N}}(A)_{i}}:=[(0,c_{0}^{A},\ldots,(i,c_{i}^{A})]

It is easy to confirm the counit and coextensions preserve the constant elements.

We then define:

•

A comonad
\mathbb{E}
as the restriction of
\mathbb{P}_{m+\mathbb{N}}
to lists of the form:

[(0,a_{0}),(1,a_{1}),\ldots,(n,a_{n})]

•

A comonad
\mathbb{M}
as the restriction of
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{N}}
to lists of the form:

[(0,a_{0}),(1,a_{1}),(0,a_{2}),\ldots,(\operatorname{parity}(n),a_{n})]

such that
E^{A}(a_{i},a_{i+1})
and
a_{0}=c^{A}_{0}
.

•

A comonad
\mathbb{P}
as being
\mathbb{P}_{m+\mathbb{N}}
.

Then, up to isomorphism as comonads:

•

The comonad
\mathbb{E}_{k}
is given by restricting
\mathbb{E}
to lists of at most
m+k
elements.

•

The comonad
\mathbb{M}_{k}
is given by restricting
\mathbb{M}
to lists of at most
1+k
elements.

•

The comonad
\mathbb{P}_{k}
is given by restricting
\mathbb{P}
to lists with pebble indices at most
m+k
.

In each case, it is routine to verify that coextensions preserve the restricted class of lists.
∎

The following is a standard result about lifting factorization systems to Eilenberg-Moore categories.
See
3

Proof sketch.

It is clear that both
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{M}}
contain all isomorphisms and are closed under compositions. The factorisation of a coalgebra morphism
h\colon(A,\alpha)\to(B,\beta)
is computed as the
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
-factorisation of the underlying morphism into a quotient
e
and an embedding
m
as shown below.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(e)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(m)}

Since
\mathbb{C}
restricts to embeddings,
\mathbb{C}(m)
is an embedding and so there exists a diagonal filler
\gamma
. It is immediate to check that
\gamma
is a
\mathbb{C}
-coalgebra.

Next, assume we have a commutative square of coalgebra morphisms, with
e\in\overline{\mathcal{E}}
and
m\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A,\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B,\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

By orthogonality of
e
and
m
, there exists a unique diagonal filler
d
. It is immediate to show that
d
is a coalgebra morphism
(B,\beta)\to(A^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
from the fact that
e
is orthogonal to
\mathbb{C}(m)
.

The “furthermore” part follows from the definitions.
∎

A.1
Proofs for section
3.1

See
7

Proof.

Recall from section 9 in
[
4
]
that
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{E}_{k})
can be identified with the category of
\sigma
-structures equipped with forest order
\sqsubseteq
of depth
{\leq}\,k
such that, for any relation symbol
R\in\sigma
,
R^{A}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
implies
x_{i}\sqsubseteq x_{j}
or
x_{j}\sqsubseteq x_{i}
(
\forall i,j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
). The morphisms in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{E}_{k})
are then the morphisms of
\sigma
-structures which preserve the covering relation
\prec
, where
x\prec y
iff
x\sqsubseteq y
and, for every
z
such that
x\sqsubseteq z\sqsubseteq y
, either
z=x
or
z=y
.

It then follows from the definition of
\mathbin{\widehat{+}}
(in terms of the equaliser) that
(A,\sqsubseteq^{A})\mathbin{\widehat{+}}(B,\sqsubseteq^{B})
consists of precisely the words
w\in\mathbb{E}_{k}(A+B)
such that

•

the restriction of
w
to the sub-word consisting of only letters from
A
, is a path in
(A,\sqsubseteq^{A})
and

•

similarly, the sub-word of
w
with letters from
B
, is a path in
(B,\sqsubseteq^{B})

From this it is easy to see that, given open pathwise embeddings
f
and
g
, also
f\mathbin{\widehat{+}}g
is an open pathwise-embedding, because checking openness and being a pathwise embeddings for
f\mathbin{\widehat{+}}g
is decomposed into the corresponding properties of
f
and
g
.
∎

A.2
Proofs for section
3.2

We shall write
g\bullet f
for composition in Kleisli categories.

See
11

Proof.

For preservation of
\leftrightarrows
, let
\overline{H}
be the lifted functor induced by
\kappa
. If for
1\leq i\leq n
,
A_{i}\leftrightarrows_{\mathbb{C}_{i}}B_{i}
, then equivalently there are
\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{C}_{i})
-morphisms
f_{i}:F_{\mathbb{C}_{i}}(A_{i})\rightarrow F_{\mathbb{C}_{i}}(B_{i})
and
g_{i}:F_{\mathbb{C}_{i}}(B_{i})\rightarrow F_{\mathbb{C}_{i}}(A_{i})
. Applying
\overline{H}
yields
\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{D})
-morphisms:

The argument for preservation of

∎

A.3
Proofs for section
3.3

The following result appears in various guises in the literature
[
24
,
40
]
, although with varying assumptions. We provide a full proof at the level of generality we require.
See
13

Proof.

\displaystyle=\iota\circ\widehat{H}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern \displaystyle=\mathbb{D}(\kappa)\circ\delta\circ\tilde{\iota}\circ\widehat{H}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern Therefore
\tilde{\iota}
is natural of type
\widehat{H}\circ\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}_{i}\Rightarrow F^{\mathbb{D}}\circ H
.
∎

See
14

Proof.

We prove the more general statement, when
\mathbb{C}
is on a wide subcategory
\mathcal{C}
of
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
. Let
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
be the (usual) proper factorisation system on
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
.

We first observe that
(\mathcal{E}\cap\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M}\cap\mathcal{C})=(\mathcal{E}\cap\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})
is a factorisation system on
\mathcal{C}
. Let
f\in\mathcal{C}
and
m\circ e
be is its factorisation in
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
. Because
\mathcal{C}
contains embeddings,
m\in\mathcal{C}
and by the last assumption we also have that
e\in\mathcal{C}
as
f=m\circ e
is in
\mathcal{C}
. The diagonality condition of factorisation systems is immediate. Consequently,
\mathsf{EM}(\mathcal{C})
is equipped with the factorisation system
(\overline{\mathcal{E}\cap\mathcal{C}},\overline{\mathcal{M}})
by lemma
3
.

Next, we observe that
\mathcal{C}
is closed under equalisers and coproducts in
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
. For the former, let
A\xrightarrow{e}B\overset{f,g}{\rightrightarrows}C
be an equaliser diagram in
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
with
f,g\in\mathcal{C}
. By lemma
A.1
.3,
e\in\mathcal{C}
. Furthermore, let
D\xrightarrow{h}B\overset{f,g}{\rightrightarrows}C
be a commutative diagram in
\mathcal{C}
and
\overline{h}\colon D\to A
the unique morphism such that
e\circ\overline{h}=h
. Then,
\overline{h}\in\mathcal{C}
by our last assumption, and therefore,
e
also an equaliser morphism of
f,g
in
\mathcal{C}
.

Observe that
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
is
\overline{\mathcal{M}}
-well-powered because
\mathcal{C}
is
\mathcal{M}
-well-powered because
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
is as well. The rest follows from proposition
A.2
below and from the fact that
\mathcal{C}
has coproducts and the forgetful functor
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})\to\mathcal{C}
reflects them.
∎

The following is essentially an abstract form of Linton’s theorem
[
31
]
, phrased using factorization systems to fit our needs.

Proposition A.2
.

If category
\mathcal{C}
:

1.

Has a proper factorization system
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
.

2.

Is
\mathcal{M}
-well-powered.

3.

Has coproducts.

Then
\mathcal{C}
has equalizers.

Proof.

Consider parallel pair:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

As
\mathcal{C}
is well-powered and has a factorization system, there is a set
I
of isomorphism classes of embeddings
m_{i}:M_{i}\rightarrow A
equalizing
f
and
g
. As

f\circ[m_{i}]=[f\circ m_{i}]=[g\circ m_{i}]=g\circ[m_{i}]

the following commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coprod_{i}M_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[m_{i}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[m_{i}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

As
\mathcal{C}
has a factorization system, the morphism
[m_{i}]
factors as:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coprod_{i}M_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

Assume
h:C\rightarrow A
equalizes
f
and
g
. There must exist
i
such that
h
factors as
m_{i}\circ e^{\prime}
. Let
j
be the corresponding coproduct injection. The following commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coprod_{i}M_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

As the factorization system is proper,
m
is a monomorphism, and so
e\circ k\circ e^{\prime}
is the unique fill in map, and the following is an equalizer diagram:

See

Proof.

For

, the relation

. Then, by definition,

See
17

Proof.

By proposition
5
,
A\equiv_{\text{FO}_{k}}B
is characterized by the existence of a span of open pathwise embeddings

F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(\mathfrak{t}^{I}A)\leftarrow R\rightarrow F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(\mathfrak{t}^{I}B)

Therefore, it suffices to check that given an
FO
-
\mathbb{E}_{k}
-smooth operation
H\colon\prod_{i}R(\sigma_{i})\rightarrow R(\tau)
and spans of open pathwise embeddings:

F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(\mathfrak{t}^{I}A_{i})\xleftarrow{f_{i}}R_{i}\xrightarrow{g_{i}}F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(\mathfrak{t}^{I}B_{i})\quad\text{in }\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{E}_{k})\text{ for $\mathbb{E}_{k}$ over $\mathcal{R}(\sigma_{i})$}

for all
1\leq i\leq n
, we can produce another span of open pathwise embeddings:

F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(\mathfrak{t}^{I}(H(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern (5)

By assumption,
H
is a

-
\mathbb{E}_{k}
-smooth operation, so there exists a natural transformation
\theta\colon H\circ\mathfrak{t}^{I}\rightarrow\mathfrak{t}^{I}\circ H
such that the components of
F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(\theta)
are open pathwise embeddings. This allows us to compute (
5
) as the following composition in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{E}_{k})
for

over
\mathcal{R}(\tau)

A.5
Proofs for section
3.5

Lemma A.3
.

If
e_{1},\dots,e_{n}
are embeddings in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C}_{1})
, …,
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C}_{n})
, respectively, then so is
\widehat{H}(e_{1},\dots,e_{n})
.

Proof.

\widehat{H}(e_{1},\dots,e_{n})

. Observe that, by lemma

,

, respectively.

The path embedding

has a minimal decomposition via

as shown above. Then, by minimality of this decomposition and by lemma
A.4
, for
i=1,\dots,n
, there exist
f^{\prime}_{i}\colon\pi_{i}\to\pi^{\prime}_{i}
and
h_{i}\colon\pi^{\prime}_{i}\to\rho_{i}
such that
f_{i}\circ e_{i}=e^{\prime}_{i}\circ f^{\prime}_{i}
and
e^{\prime}_{i}=g_{i}\circ h_{i}
. Since
f_{i}
is open and
f_{i}\circ e_{i}=g_{i}\circ h_{i}\circ f^{\prime}_{i}
, there is a morphism
d_{i}\colon\rho_{i}\to\alpha_{i}
such that
d_{i}\circ h_{i}\circ f^{\prime}_{i}=e_{i}
and
g_{i}=d_{i}\circ f_{i}
. Finally, because the outer rectangle and the bottom rectangle commute and
\widehat{H}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle g_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern is a mono, the top rectangle commute as well. Consequently,
\widehat{H}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern ∎

A.6
Multilinear maps

We introduce a class of maps between coalgebras of use when checking
(S1)
and
(S2)
axioms, and establish a connection to functors lifted to Eilenberg-Moore categories.

For a Kleisli-law
\kappa
,
\mathbb{D}
-coalgebra
(A,\alpha)
, and
\mathbb{C}_{i}
-coalgebras
(B_{i},\beta_{i})
, we shall say that a
\mathcal{D}
-morphism
h:A\rightarrow H(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle B_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle B_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle B_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle B_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}})
is a
multilinear map

\alpha\to[\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\beta_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\beta_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern The following result appears in various guises in the literature for the dual setting of monads
[
24
,
40
]
. We provide a full proof as similar results often appear restricted to the binary setting, and in relation to commutative monads. Our proof clarifies that the additional assumptions of a commutative (co)monad serve no role in the properties we require. We state the formulation suitable subsequent developments.

Proposition A.5
.

For a Kleisli-law
\kappa
, let
\iota
be the universal equalizer morphism appearing in the construction of
\widehat{H}
. The natural transformation:

.
That is, any multilinear map
f\colon\beta\rightarrow[\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern factors through

via a unique

-coalgebra morphism

Proof.

We first establish
\varepsilon\circ\iota
is multilinear. Let
\omega
be the structure map of
\widehat{H}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\alpha_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\alpha_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}})

\displaystyle\kappa\circ\mathbb{D}(\varepsilon)\circ\mathbb{D}(\iota)\circ\omega

\displaystyle=\kappa\circ\mathbb{D}(\varepsilon)\circ\delta\circ\iota

\displaystyle=\kappa\circ\varepsilon\circ\delta\circ\iota

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\circ\mathbb{D}(\kappa)\circ\delta\circ\iota

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\circ\mathbb{D}H(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern \displaystyle=H(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern be a coalgebra, and

a multilinear map. Then

is a coalgebra morphism by:

\mathbb{D}^{2}(f)\circ\mathbb{D}(\beta)\circ\beta=\mathbb{D}^{2}(f)\circ\delta\circ\beta=\delta\circ\mathbb{D}(f)\circ\beta.

Further, it equalizes
F^{\mathbb{D}}(H(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern and

, as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}(H(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern \displaystyle=\mathbb{D}(\kappa)\circ\mathbb{D}^{2}(f)\circ\mathbb{D}(\beta)\circ\beta

be the unique coalgebra morphism given by the equalizer universal property by

.

2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\alpha_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\alpha_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}})
, then
\varepsilon\circ\iota\circ g
is a multilinear map as:

\displaystyle H(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\alpha_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\alpha_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\alpha_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\alpha_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}})\circ\varepsilon\circ\iota\circ g

\displaystyle=\kappa\circ\mathbb{D}(\varepsilon\circ\iota)\circ\omega\circ g

\displaystyle=\kappa\circ\mathbb{D}(\varepsilon\circ\iota)\circ\mathbb{D}(g)\circ\beta

\displaystyle=\kappa\circ\mathbb{D}(\varepsilon\circ\iota\circ g)\circ\beta

Then combining these two operations in one direction:

\varepsilon\circ\iota\circ\tilde{f}=\varepsilon\circ\mathbb{D}(f)\circ\beta=f\circ\varepsilon\circ\beta=f

In the other direction, we claim
\widetilde{\varepsilon\circ\iota\circ f}=f
. As:

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}(\varepsilon\circ\iota\circ f)\circ\beta

\displaystyle=\mathbb{D}(\varepsilon)\circ\mathbb{D}(\iota)\circ\mathbb{D}(f)\circ\beta

\displaystyle=\mathbb{D}(\varepsilon)\circ\mathbb{D}(\iota)\circ\omega\circ f

\displaystyle=\mathbb{D}(\varepsilon)\circ\delta\circ\iota\circ f

\displaystyle=\iota\circ f

The equalizer universal property then establishes the claimed equality. The two mappings then exhibit the required bijection.
∎

Remark A.6
.

Our multilinear map terminology is connected to an instance of the dual construction in monad theory. See for example
[
24
,
40
]
, where the corresponding binary notion (called “bilinearity”) is phrased in terms of Kleisli maps.

.

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}

f
we know that the outer square commutes. Further, the two squares on the right commute by naturality of
\kappa
and the assumption that
e_{i}
, for
1\leq i\leq n

2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\mathbb{D}(e_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{D}(e_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}})\circ\kappa\circ\mathbb{D}(f_{0})\circ\alpha
. Since
\mathbb{D}
and
H
preserve embeddings,
H(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\mathbb{D}(e_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\mathbb{D}(e_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\mathbb{D}(e_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{D}(e_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}})
is an embedding and hence a mono, proving multilinearity of
f_{0}
.
∎

Next, we translate axiom

in terms of multilinear maps, which makes it easier to work with.

.

A.7
Proofs for section
3.6

See
21

Proof.

Axioms
(F1)
and
(F2)
follow automatically because the underlying function of
\kappa
is the identity function.

Next, we check that
\kappa
satisfies the smoothness axioms
(S1)
and
(S2’)
from lemma
A.8
.
For
(S1)
, we see that
{\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R}}
preserves embeddings
e:A\rightarrow B
, as it follows that
R^{{\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R}}(A)}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r})
if and only if
R^{{\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R}}(B)}({\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R}}(e)(a_{1}),\dots,{\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R}}(e)(a_{r}))
from
R^{A}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r})\Leftrightarrow R^{B}(e(a_{1}),\dots,e(a_{r}))
and also
P^{A}_{i_{z}}(a_{z})\Leftrightarrow P^{B}_{i_{z}}(e(a_{z}))
for all
z\leq r
.

For
(S2’)
, suppose
\pi\colon P\to\mathbb{E}_{k}P
is a path
r_{1}\sqsubset_{\pi}r_{2}\sqsubset_{\pi}\dots\sqsubset_{\pi}r_{n}
and
f\colon\pi\to[\alpha]
is a multilinear map. Because
\kappa
is just the identity function, multilinearity of
f
translates as

\displaystyle\alpha(f(r_{i}))=\kappa([f(r_{1}),\dots,f(r_{n})])=[f(r_{1}),\dots,f(r_{n})],

(7)

for every
i=1,\dots,n
. In other words,
f(r_{1}),\dots,f(r_{n})
is a path in
\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}
(recall remark
2
). Therefore, the inclusion
j\colon P^{\prime}\to A
of the induced substructure on
\{f(r_{1}),\dots,f(r_{n})\}
into
A
, is a path embedding
\pi^{\prime}\to\alpha
where
\pi^{\prime}
is the restriction of
\alpha
to
P
.
Further, the mapping
f
factors as
{\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R}}(j)\circ f_{0}
where
f_{0}
is a homomorphism because
f
is and
j
is an embedding.
Minimality is immediate from the fact that the image of
P
under
f
is onto
P^{\prime}
.
∎

A.8
Proofs for section
3.7

We first justify that
\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}
is a comonad. Viewing
\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}(A)
as an induced substructure of
\mathbb{E}_{k}(A)
, we see that the restriction of
\varepsilon\colon\mathbb{E}_{k}(A)\to A
to
\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}(A)
is a morphism in
\mathcal{R}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma)
. Further, defining coextensions
(-)^{*}
as for
\mathbb{E}_{k}
we see that, for any morphism
f\colon\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}(A)\to B
in
\mathcal{R}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma)
, the coextension
f^{*}
is also a morphism in
\mathcal{R}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma)
. The axioms (
1
) hold for
(\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}},\varepsilon,(-)^{*})
because they already hold for
\mathbb{E}_{k}
.

Observe that
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}})
has equalisers of reflexive pairs. This follows from lemma
14
, it is easy to see that its assumptions are satisfied for
\mathbb{C}=\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}
and
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{R}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma_{3})
. Therefore, if
\kappa
is a Kleisli law, the functor
\mathbin{\parallel}
lifts to a functor
\mathbin{\widehat{\mathbin{\parallel}}}\colon\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}})\times\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}})\to\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}})
. The following shows that this is the case and that the lifted functor preserves open pathwise embeddings.

See
22

Proof.

We check that
\kappa
is a well-defined natural transformation. First, to see that each component of
\kappa
is a well-defined
\sigma
-structure homomorphism, assume that
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in R^{\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}(A\mathbin{\parallel}B)}
holds for some
R
in
\sigma
and
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
in
\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}(A\mathbin{\parallel}B)
. By definition of
\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}(A\mathbin{\parallel}B)
,
\varepsilon(x_{i})=\iota(y_{i})
, for some
(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})\in R^{A}
or
(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})\in R^{B}
. W.l.o.g. assume that the former is the case.
By definition of
\kappa
, each
\kappa(x_{i})
is either
\mathtt{ex}_{1}(x_{i})\in\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}(A)
or a constant
c_{i}
. Therefore,
\kappa(x_{i})=\mathfrak{i}(z_{i})
for some
z_{i}\in\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}(A)
and, moreover,
\varepsilon(z_{i})=y_{i}
, for
1\leq i\leq n
. Finally, we see that non-constant
x_{i}
are comparable in the prefix order in
\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}(A\mathbin{\parallel}B)
and this property is preserved by
\mathtt{ex}_{1}(-)
. Therefore,
(\kappa(x_{1}),\dots,\kappa(x_{n}))\in R^{\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}(A)\mathbin{\parallel}\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}(B)}
.

Naturality of
\kappa
is immediate from the fact that morphisms in
\mathcal{R}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma_{1})
and
\mathcal{R}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma_{1})
send constant elements to constant elements and non-constant elements to non-constant elements.

Next, we check the smoothness axioms. Observe that
(F1)
and
(S1)
hold by definitions. Further,
(F2)
follows by an easy case analysis.
For
(S2’)
from lemma
A.8
, let
f\colon\pi\to[\alpha,\beta]
be a multilinear map from a path
\pi
in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}})
and let
r_{1}\sqsubset_{\pi}r_{2}\sqsubset_{\pi}\dots\sqsubset_{\pi}r_{n}
be the chain of non-constant elements in
\pi
. Multilinearity of
f
implies

\kappa([f(r_{1}),\dots,f(r_{i})])=(\alpha\mathbin{\parallel}\beta)(f(r_{i})),\quad\text{for $i=1,\dots,n$.}

(8)

In fact, this expresses precisely that if
f(r_{i})\in\mathfrak{i}(A)
then
\mathtt{ex}_{1}([f(r_{1}),\dots,f(r_{i})])
is a path in
\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}
and, dually,
f(r_{i})\in\mathfrak{i}(B)
implies that
\mathtt{ex}_{2}([f(r_{1}),\dots,f(r_{i})])
is a path in
\sqsubseteq_{\beta}
. From this it immediately follows that
f
factors through the embedding

e_{1}\mathbin{\parallel}e_{2}\colon P\mathbin{\parallel}Q\rightarrowtail A\mathbin{\parallel}B

where
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
are the path embeddings corresponding to the paths
\mathtt{ex}_{1}([f(r_{1}),\dots,f(r_{n})])
and
\mathtt{ex}_{2}([f(r_{1}),\dots,f(r_{n})])
, respectively. Minimality of
P
and
Q
is immediate.

We remark that
\kappa
is smooth by the above discussion, theorem
20
and lemma
A.8
.
∎

Lemma A.9
.

For
\sigma
-structures
A,B
,
\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A)\leftrightarrow_{\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}}\mathfrak{t}^{I}(B)
iff
A\equiv_{\text{FO}_{k}}B

Proof.

The proof of proposition
5
can be adapted to this statement. Moreover, we prove a general version for multi-sorted first order logic in proposition
27
.
∎

See
23

Proof.

We need to show that
\mathbin{\parallel}
, viewed as an operation
\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma_{1})\times\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma_{2})\to\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma_{3})
is
FO
-
\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}
-smooth. We defined
\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}
as a comonad on
\mathcal{R}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma)
, for any
\sigma
, i.e. including
\sigma=\sigma^{I}_{1},\sigma^{I}_{2}
, or
\sigma^{I}_{3}
. Also, the usual
\mathfrak{t}^{I}\colon\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma)\to\mathcal{R}(\sigma^{I})

followed by the inclusion
\mathcal{R}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma^{I})\subseteq\mathcal{R}(\sigma^{I})

and

shown below.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma_{1})\times\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma^{I}_{1})\times\mathcal{R}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma^{I}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma^{I}_{1})\times\mathcal{R}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma^{I}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma_{3})}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{t}_{1}\times\mathfrak{t}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbin{\parallel}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbin{\parallel}^{I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}\times\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbin{\parallel}^{I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{t}_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}}

(9)

Where
\mathbin{\parallel}^{I}

. It is easy to observe that
\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A)\mathbin{\mathbin{\parallel}^{I}}\mathfrak{t}^{I}(B)\cong\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A\mathbin{\parallel}B)

.

A.9
Proofs for section

Proof.

For
A\in\mathcal{R}^{S}(\sigma)
, the morphism
\varepsilon_{A}\colon\mathbb{E}_{k}A\rightarrow A
is defined component-wise:

(\varepsilon_{A})_{s}(c^{\mathbb{E}_{k}A})=c^{A}\text{ for }c\in\mathbf{cs}(\sigma,s)\quad(\varepsilon_{A})_{s}([a_{1}{:}s_{1},\dots,a_{n}{:}s_{n}])=a_{n}\text{ with }s=s_{n}

For a
\sigma
-morphism
f\colon\mathbb{E}_{k}A\rightarrow B
, the morphism
f^{*}\colon\mathbb{E}_{k}A\rightarrow\mathbb{E}_{k}B
is defined component-wise:

(f^{*})_{s}(c^{\mathbb{E}_{k}A})=c^{\mathbb{E}_{k}B}\text{ for }c\in\mathbf{cs}(\sigma,s)\quad(f^{*})_{s}([a_{1}{:}s_{1},\dots,a_{n}{:}s_{n}])=[b_{1}{:}s_{1},\dots,b_{n}{:}s_{n}]

where
b_{i}=f_{s_{i}}([a_{1}{:}s_{1},\dots,a_{i}{:}s_{i}])]
for
1\leq i\leq n
and
s=s_{n}
.
Verifying that
\varepsilon_{A}
and
f^{*}
are
\sigma
-morphisms is straightforward application of the definitions. Moreover, verifying the axioms (
1
) is also routine.

In order to show
\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}
is comonad, we note that for all
A\in\mathcal{R}^{S}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma)
,

is a morphism in
\mathcal{R}^{S}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma)
and that

is a morphism in
\mathcal{R}^{S}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma)
whenever

\mathcal{R}^{S}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma)
.
