See
27

We prove that bisimulation for the
\mathfrak{t}^{J}
-lifting
S
-sorted
\mathbb{E}_{k}
captures equivalence in
\text{FO}_{k}(S,J)
, by first introducing the corresponding Ehrenfeucht–Fraïssé game
\mathbf{EF}^{J}_{k}(A,B)
and showing that this captures the equivalence in
\text{FO}_{k}(S,J)
by an adaptation of the standard single-sorted proof found in chapter three of
[
30
]
. We then prove that the existence of a Duplicator winning strategy in
\mathbf{EF}^{J}_{k}(A,B)
is equivalent to the existence of open pathwise embeddings
F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}\mathfrak{t}^{J}A\leftarrow(W,\upsilon)\rightarrow F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}\mathfrak{t}^{J}B
by adapting the single-sorted proof found in
[
4
]
.
Later we explain how to adapt the proof to obtain also the second item of the statement.

Generalising the single-sorted case, every move of the
\mathbf{EF}^{J}_{k}(A,B)
game results in a
S
-sorted relation
\gamma
of type
A
to
B
. A
S
-sorted relation
\gamma
from
S
-sorted set
A
to
S
-sorted set
B
is an
S
-indexed family of sets
\gamma_{s}\subseteq A_{s}\times B_{s}
. Since the logic
\text{FO}_{k}(S,J)
has equality only for terms of sort
s\in J
, the winning condition of
\mathbf{EF}^{J}_{k}(A,B)
requires a notion of
S
-sorted relation
\gamma
which is only required to be single-valued, i.e. a partial function, on sorts
s\in J
. The following notion is used to define the winning moves in
\mathbf{EF}^{J}_{k}(A,B)
:

Definition A.10
.

Given
S
-sorted
\sigma
structures
A
,
B
and
J\subseteq S
, a
S
-sorted relation
\gamma
of type
A\times B
is a partial isomorphism on
J
from
A
to
B
if the following conditions hold:

•

If
c\in\mathbf{cs}(\sigma,s)
, then
(c^{A},c^{B})\in\gamma_{s}

•

For all
s\in J
,
\gamma_{s}
is a partial function from
A_{s}
to
B_{s}

•

For every
R\in\sigma
of arity
\langle s_{1},\dots,s_{m}\rangle
,

R^{A}(a_{1},\dots,a_{m})\Leftrightarrow R^{B}(b_{1},\dots,b_{m})

whenever
(a_{1},b_{1})\in\gamma_{s_{1}},\dots,(a_{m},b_{m})\in\gamma_{s_{m}}

We are now able to introduce the
k
-round many-sorted Ehrenfeucht–Fraïssé game:

Definition A.11
.

Given
S
-sorted
\sigma
structures
A
and
B
, the
k
-round
S
-sorted Ehrenfeucht-Fraïssé game
\mathbf{EF}^{J}_{k}(A,B)
with equality on
J
is played between two players, Spoiler and Duplicator. In each round
i\leq k
,

•

Spoiler chooses a sort
s_{i}\in S
and element
a_{i}\in A_{s_{i}}
or
b_{i}\in B_{s_{i}}
.

•

Duplicator responds with an element
b_{i}\in B_{s_{i}}
or
a_{i}\in A_{s_{i}}
in the other structure’s
s_{i}
sort.

Duplicator wins round
i
, if the following
S
-sorted relation
\gamma
, defined componentwise,

\gamma_{i,s}=\{(c^{A},c^{B})\mid c\in\mathbf{cs}(\sigma,s)\}\cup\{(a_{j},b_{j})\mid\text{ for }s_{j}=s\text{ and }j\leq i\}

is a partial isomorphism on
J
from
A
to
B
.

Duplicator is said to have a winning strategy in
\mathbf{EF}^{J}_{k}(A,B)
if for all possible Spoiler moves in the
k
-round game, Duplicator can win round
i
for all
i\leq k
.

As expected, the
\mathbf{EF}^{J}_{k}(A,B)
game captures equivalence in
\text{FO}_{k}(S,J)
. To prove this, we consider structures
(A,a)
in the expanded signature
\sigma(c{:}s)
. The signature
\sigma(c{:}s)
has an additional fresh constant symbol
c
added to
\mathbf{cs}(\sigma,s)
, the same constants as
\mathbf{cs}(\sigma,s^{\prime})
for
s^{\prime}\not=s
, and the same relation symbols as
\sigma
. The notation
(A,a)
is the
\sigma(c{:}s)
-structure where
A
is a
\sigma
-structure and
c^{A}=a\in A_{s}
.

Proposition A.12
.

The following are equivalent

1.

Duplicator has a winning strategy in
\mathbf{EF}^{J}_{k}(A,B)

2.

A\equiv_{\text{FO}_{k}(S,J)}B

Proof.

(1)\Rightarrow(2)
We prove the statement by induction on
k
. Suppose
k=0
and that Duplicator has a winning strategy in
\mathbf{EF}^{J}_{0}(A,B)
. By the winning condition, the relation
\gamma
, defined componentwise as
{\gamma_{s}=\{(c^{A},c^{B})\mid c\in\mathbf{cs}(\sigma,s)\}}
is a partial isomorphism. In particular,
\gamma
is a partial isomorphism on
J
. Hence, from the definition of partial isomorphism,
A
and
B
satisfy the same atomic sentences.

For the inductive step, suppose Duplicator has a winning strategy in
\mathbf{EF}^{J}_{k+1}(A,B)
and consider the sentence
\phi
of rank
k+1
. It follows from the definition of quantifier rank, that every sentence
\phi
of rank
k+1
is a boolean combination of sentences of the form
\exists y{:}s\psi(y{:}s)
for sort
s\in S
where
\psi
is a formula of rank
\leq k
. Hence, by recursion on the construction of formulas
\phi(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}})
, it suffices to only prove that
A\vDash\phi\Leftrightarrow B\vDash\phi
for
\phi=\exists y{:}s\psi(y{:}s)
.

Suppose
A\vDash\exists y{:}s\psi(y{:}s)
, then there exists some
a\in A_{s}
, such that
{(A,a)\vDash\psi(y{:}s)}
. Consider the
\mathbf{EF}^{J}_{k+1}(A,B)
game where Spoiler plays
s\in S
and
a\in A_{s}
in the first round. Let Duplicator’s response be
b\in B_{s}
according to the winning strategy. Duplicator then has a winning strategy in the
\mathbf{EF}^{J}_{k}((A,a),(B,b))
game by playing according the subsequent
k
moves of the
\mathbf{EF}_{k+1}(A,B)
game. Hence, by the inductive hypothesis, we have that for all
\sigma(c{:}s)
sentences
\phi^{\prime}
of rank
\leq k
,
(A,a)\vDash\phi^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow(B,b)\vDash\phi^{\prime}
. In particular,
(B,b)\vDash\psi(y{:}s)
. Hence,
B\vDash\exists y{:}s\psi(y{:}s)
. To show the other direction, i.e.
B\vDash\exists y{:}s\psi(y{:}s)\Rightarrow A\vDash\exists y{:}s\psi(y{:}s)
, is essentially the same.

(2)\Rightarrow(1)
We prove the statement by induction on
k
. For
k=0
,
A
and
B
satisfying the same atomic sentences proves that
\gamma
, formed from just the pairs of constants, is a partial isomorphism on
J
.

For the inductive step, assume
A
and
B
satisfy the same sentences of rank
k+1
. We need to show that Duplicator has a winning strategy in the
\mathbf{EF}_{k+1}(A,B)
game. Suppose, in the first round of
k+1
, Spoiler chooses
s\in S
and
a\in A_{s}
. There is finite collection of in-equivalent formulas
\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{M}
of rank
k
with variable
x{:}s
such that
(A,a)\vDash\varphi_{i}(x{:}s)
. Hence,
A\vDash\exists x{:}s(\bigwedge_{i\leq M}\varphi_{i}(x{:}s))
. The sentence
\exists x{:}s(\bigwedge_{i\leq M}\varphi_{i}(x{:}s))
has rank
k+1
, so by the supposition,
B\vDash\exists x{:}s(\bigwedge_{i\leq M}\varphi_{i}(x{:}s))
. Therefore, there exists a
b\in B_{s}
witnessing the existential quantifier
\exists x{:}s
; and so Duplicator responds with this
b\in B_{s}
. By construction,
{(A,a)\vDash\varphi(x{:}s)\Leftrightarrow(B,b)\vDash\varphi(x{:}s)}
for all
\sigma(c)
-sentences of rank
\leq k
. By the inductive hypothesis, Duplicator has a winning strategy in the
\mathbf{EF}_{k}((A,a),(B,b))
game. Therefore, Duplicator proceeding in the subsequent
k
moves of
\mathbf{EF}_{k+1}(A,B)
according to the moves of
\mathbf{EF}_{k}((A,a),(B,b))
yields a winning strategy for
\mathbf{EF}_{k+1}(A,B)
.
∎

Proposition A.13
.

The following are equivalent:

1.

Duplicator has a winning strategy in
\mathbf{EF}^{J}_{k}(A,B)

2.

\mathfrak{t}^{J}A\leftrightarrow_{\mathbb{E}_{k}}\mathfrak{t}^{J}B

Proof.

(1)\Rightarrow(2)
Suppose Duplicator has a winning strategy in
\mathbf{EF}^{J}_{k}(A,B)
, this determines a
S
-sorted set
W
with components
W_{s}\subseteq(\mathbb{E}_{k}A)_{s}\times(\mathbb{E}_{k}B)_{s}
that satisfy the following conditions:

•

For all
c\in\mathbf{cs}(\sigma,s)
,
(c^{\mathbb{E}_{k}A},c^{\mathbb{E}_{k}B})\in W_{s}

•

(forth) For every pair
(w,v)\in W_{s}
such that
w
is a word of length
<k
or a pair of empty words
(w,v)
and
a\in A_{s^{\prime}}
(Spoiler’s move), there exists a
b\in B_{s^{\prime}}
(Duplicator’s response) such that
(w[a{:}s^{\prime}],v[b{:}s^{\prime}])\in W_{s^{\prime}}
.

•

(back) For every pair
(w,v)\in W_{s}
such that
v
is a word of length
<k
or a pair of empty words
(w,v)
and
b\in B_{s^{\prime}}
, there exists a
a\in A_{s^{\prime}}
such that

(w[a{:}s^{\prime}],v[b{:}s^{\prime}])\in W_{s^{\prime}}

•

(winning) The relation
\gamma_{w,v}
where
(\gamma_{w,v})_{s}
consists of pairs
(a,b)
such that
a{:}s
and
b{:}s
appear at the same index in
w
and
v
(respectively) is partial isomorphism on
J
for every pair
(w,v)
in
S
-sorted set
W
.

Since
W
is a
S
-sorted subset of
\mathbb{E}_{k}\mathfrak{t}^{J}A\times\mathbb{E}_{k}\mathfrak{t}^{J}B
, we can consider the induced substructure
W
of this product structure. This structure
W
has a natural coalgebra
\omega:W\rightarrow\mathbb{E}_{k}W
where constants are mapped to constants and
\omega_{s}(w_{n},v_{n})
is mapped to the sequence
[(w_{1},v_{1}){:}s_{1},\dots,(w_{n},v_{n}){:}s_{n}]
where
w_{1}\sqsubset_{\delta_{A}}\dots\sqsubset_{\delta_{A}}w_{n}
and
v_{1}\sqsubset_{\delta_{B}}\dots\sqsubset_{\delta_{B}}v_{n}
are the unique chains below in
w_{n}
and
v_{n}
in
F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}\mathfrak{t}^{J}A
and
F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}\mathfrak{t}^{J}B
, respectively.
The projection maps

and
p_{2}:(W,\omega)\rightarrow F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}\mathfrak{t}^{J}B
are coalgebra morphisms, but we must show that these projection maps are also open pathwise embeddings.

To verify

is a pathwise embedding, we note that the image of path embedding
e:(P,\pi)\rightarrow(W,\omega)
contains elements
(w_{1},v_{1}){:}s_{1},\dots,(w_{n},v_{n}){:}s_{n}
where
w_{1}\sqsubset_{\delta_{A}}\dots\sqsubset_{\delta_{A}}w_{n}
and

are chains in
\mathbb{E}_{k}\mathfrak{t}^{J}A
and
\mathbb{E}_{k}\mathfrak{t}^{J}B
, respectively. As

is a path in
\mathbb{E}_{k}\mathfrak{t}^{J}A
, we have that
p_{1}\circ e:(P,\pi)\rightarrow\mathbb{E}_{k}\mathfrak{t}^{J}A
is a path embedding.

To verify
p_{1}:(W,\omega)\rightarrow F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}\mathfrak{t}^{J}A
is open, consider the following commuting diagram in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{E}_{k})
:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(P,\pi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Q,\chi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(W,\omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}\mathfrak{t}^{J}A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}

where
e_{0}:(P,\pi)\rightarrowtail(W,\omega)
and
e_{1}:(Q,\chi)\rightarrowtail F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}\mathfrak{t}^{J}A
are path embeddings. Suppose
w_{1}\sqsubset_{\delta_{A}}\dots\sqsubset_{\delta_{A}}w_{n}
is the image of path
Q
presented as
r_{1}\sqsubset_{\chi}\dots\sqsubset_{\chi}r_{n}
under embedding
e_{1}
, then by the above diagram commuting
(w_{1},v_{1})\sqsubset_{\omega}\dots\sqsubset_{\omega}(w_{i},v_{i})
for some

and
v_{1}\sqsubset_{\delta_{B}}\dots\sqsubset_{\delta_{B}}v_{i}
in
\mathbb{E}_{k}\mathfrak{t}^{J}B
. We need to construct an embedding

that ‘fills the diagonal’.

Suppose
w_{j}=w_{i}[a_{i+1}{:}s_{i+1},\dots,a_{j}{:}s_{j}]
such that
w_{j}
are the elements in the image of
e_{1}
, but not in the image of

. By repeated applications of the forth property, we obtain pairs
(w_{j},v_{j})\in W_{s}
with
v_{j}=v_{i}[b_{i+1}{:}s_{i+1},\dots,b_{j}{:}s_{j}]
. Let

for all

. By the winning condition on

,

is indeed an embedding. Hence, the following diagram commutes:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(P,\pi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Q,\chi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(W,\omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}\mathfrak{t}^{J}A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}

A similar proof shows that
p_{2}:(W,\omega)\rightarrow F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}B
is a open pathwise embedding.

(2)\Rightarrow(1)
Suppose we have span of open pathwise embeddings
F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}\mathfrak{t}^{J}A\xleftarrow{h_{1}}(W,\omega)\xrightarrow{h_{2}}F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}\mathfrak{t}^{J}B
. We prove the following statement by induction on
n\leq k
:

(*)
For every
s\in S
and
b\in B_{s}
, there exists a path
P
consisting of constants
\mathbf{cs}(P)
and a chain

with embedding

and

such that:

•

\varepsilon_{A}(h_{1,s}(d_{s}(r_{n})))=a
and
\varepsilon_{B}(h_{2,s}(d_{s}(r_{n})))=b

•

The
S
-sorted relation
\gamma
defined componetwise:

\displaystyle\gamma_{n,t}

\displaystyle=\{(c^{A},c^{B})\mid c\in\mathbf{cs}(\sigma,t)\}

\displaystyle\cup\{(a_{j},b_{j})\mid\text{ for $r_{j}\in P_{t}$ and $j\leq n$}\}

where
a_{j}=h_{1,t}(d_{t}(r_{j}))
and
b_{j}=h_{2,t}(d_{t}(r_{j}))

is a partial isomorphism on
J
.

For the base case

, since
h_{1}:(W,\omega)\rightarrow F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}\mathfrak{t}^{J}A
and
h_{2}:(W,\omega)\rightarrow F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}\mathfrak{t}^{J}B
are
\sigma
-morphism,
h_{1}
and

is a partial isomorphism (and in particular, a partial isomorphism on

). We set

to be the

-length chain containing only interpretations for the constants in

.

For the inductive step, suppose

and

. By the inductive hypothesis, there exists a path
(P,\pi)
containing constants and a chain

with embedding

. Consider the extension of this path to
(Q,\chi)
where

for

and

. We define
\chi
componentwise where
\chi_{s^{\prime}}=\pi_{s^{\prime}}
for all

and
\chi_{s}(r)=\pi_{s}(r)
for

,
\chi_{s}(r_{n+1})=[r_{1}{:}s_{1},\dots,r_{n}{:}s_{n},r_{n+1}{:}s]
. We can define an embedding

such that

for

,

for all

and

. By construction, we have that the following diagram commutes:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(P,\pi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Q,\chi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(W,\omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}\mathfrak{t}^{J}B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{2}}

Hence, by
h_{2}
being a open map, there exists an embedding
f:(Q,\chi)\rightarrowtail(W,\omega)
. To verify that
\gamma_{n,t}
is partial isomorphism on

, we note that by
h_{1},h_{2}
pathwise embeddings, we have that
h_{1}\circ f
and

are embeddings. Therefore:

\displaystyle R^{\mathfrak{t}^{J}A}(a_{i_{1}},\dots,a_{i_{m}})

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow R^{\mathfrak{t}^{J}A}(\varepsilon_{A}(h_{1,s_{1}}(f(r_{i_{1}}))),\dots,\varepsilon_{A}(h_{1,s_{m}}(f(r_{i_{m}}))))

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow R^{\mathbb{E}_{k}\mathfrak{t}^{J}A}(h_{1,s_{1}}(f(r_{i_{1}})),\dots,h_{1,s_{m}}(f(r_{i_{m}})))

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow R^{Q}(r_{i_{1}},\dots,r_{i_{m}})

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow R^{\mathbb{E}_{k}\mathfrak{t}^{J}B}(h_{2,s_{1}}(f(r_{i_{1}})),\dots,h_{2,s_{m}}(f(r_{i_{m}})))

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow R^{\mathfrak{t}^{J}B}(\varepsilon_{B}(h_{2,s_{1}}(f(r_{i_{1}}))),\dots,\varepsilon_{B}(h_{2,s_{m}}(f(r_{i_{m}}))))

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow R^{\mathfrak{t}^{J}B}(b_{i_{1}},\dots,b_{i_{m}})

for all relations of
R\in\sigma^{J}
of arity
R
. In particular, this equivalence holds for the relations
I_{s}\in\sigma^{J}
. Since
I
is interpreted as equality in
\mathfrak{t}^{J}A
and
\mathfrak{t}^{J}B
, we have that
\gamma_{n,t}
is a partial isomorphism on
J
.

Now to show that Duplicator has winning strategy in
\mathbf{EF}_{k}(A,B)
, suppose at the
n
-th round Spoiler chooses
s
and
b\in B_{s}
, then by
(*)
, Duplicator can respond with
a\in A_{s}
producing a partial isomorphism

on

. If Spoiler chooses

and

, we can prove a statement similar to

which implies that Duplicator can respond with a

that results in a partial isomorphism on

.
∎

Proposition
A.12
and
A.13
conclude the proof of the first item of proposition
27
, demonstrating that
\equiv_{\text{FO}_{k}(S,J)}
is captured by the many-sorted
\mathbb{E}_{k}

For the next part, we use the following fact, which allows us to reason about
\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}
-coalgebras the same way as about
\mathbb{E}_{k}
-coalgebras.

Lemma A.14
.

Given a many-sorted signature
\sigma
, and the comonads
\mathbb{E}_{k}
and
\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}
on
\mathcal{R}^{S}(\sigma)
and
\mathcal{R}^{S}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma)
, respectively, the categories
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{E}_{k})
and
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}})
are isomorphic.

Moreover, the factorisation systems (surjective, strong mono) on
\mathcal{R}^{S}(\sigma)
and
\mathcal{R}^{S}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma)
lift to the identical factorisation system on both categories of coalgebras.

Proof.

We check that every morphism involved in the definition
A\leftrightarrow_{\mathbb{E}_{k}}B
exists in the category
\mathcal{R}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma)
. First, observe that the embedding of categories
I\colon\mathcal{R}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
lifts to an embedding
\widehat{I}\colon\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}})\hookrightarrow\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{E}_{k})
. Indeed, the obvious comonad morphism
I\circ\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}\to\mathbb{E}_{k}\circ I
introduces a functor
\widehat{I}
, mapping every coalgebra
A\to\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}A
to
A\to\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}A\hookrightarrow\mathbb{E}_{k}A
. Moreover,
\widehat{I}
is surjective on objects (i.e.
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}})
is a wide subcategory of
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{E}_{k})
), because every coalgebra
A\to\mathbb{E}_{k}A
is uniquely determined by its order
\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}
on non-constants via the mapping
a\mapsto[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]
where
[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]
is the chain of non-constant elements below
a
(with
x_{n}=a
). This chain only lists non-constants and therefore factors through
\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}(A)\subseteq\mathbb{E}_{k}(A)
. Moreover, the underlying homomorphism of every coalgebra morphism in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{E}_{k})
is in
\mathcal{R}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma)
(i.e.
\widehat{I}
is an isomorphism of categories).

Next, observe the following.

1.

\mathcal{R}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma)
contains all embeddings: this is automatic by the definition of
\mathcal{R}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma)
and the fact that embeddings are injective.

2.

\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}
preserves embeddings.

3.

The factorisation system (surjective, strong injective) in
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
restricts to
\mathcal{R}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma)
.

By (1), (2), (3) and lemma
3
,
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}})
consists of the same quotients and embeddings as in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{E}_{k})
.
∎

For the second item of proposition
27
we remark that the coalgebra
(W,v)
in the proof of proposition
A.13
can be be restricted to
\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}
, i.e. plays where constants are never selected by Spoiler nor Duplicator. Conversely, we know that in the presence of equality, the action of Spoiler and Duplicator are determined when playing constants. So a span of open pathwise embeddings
F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}}(\mathfrak{t}^{J}_{\text{nc}}A)\leftarrow R\rightarrow F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}}(\mathfrak{t}^{J}_{\text{nc}}B)
contains a full strategy for the
\mathbf{EF}^{J}_{k}(A,B)
game. Hence, the construction proceeds as in the direction (2)
\Rightarrow
(1) of the proof of proposition
A.13
.

A.10
Proofs for section
4.2

See
28

We prove that bisimulation for the
\mathfrak{t}^{e}
-lifting of
S
-sorted equivalence captures equivalence in
\text{MSO}_{k}
, by first noting the functor
\mathfrak{t}^{e}
decomposes as
\mathfrak{t}^{J}\circ\mathfrak{u}^{e}
where
J=\{\mathbf{f}\}
and
\mathfrak{u}^{e}\colon\mathcal{R}(\sigma)\rightarrow\mathcal{R}(\sigma^{e}\setminus\{I\})
is functor from
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
to a category of
S
-sorted
\sigma^{e}
-structures
\mathcal{R}(\sigma^{e}\setminus\{I\})
without the
I
-relation. For a
\sigma
-structure
A
,
\mathfrak{u}^{e}(A)
is the
\sigma^{e}\setminus\{I\}
reduct of
\mathfrak{t}^{e}(A)
. We then prove the following lemma by adapting the translation detailed in footnote 12 of
[
42
]
which translates full second-order logic into many-sorted first-order logic:

Lemma A.15
.

•

For every
\text{MSO}_{k}
sentence
\phi
in signature
\sigma
, there exists a
\text{FO}_{k}(S,J)
sentence
F(\phi)
in signature
\sigma^{e}\setminus\{I\}
such that

A\vDash\phi\Leftrightarrow\mathfrak{u}^{e}A\vDash F(\phi)

•

For every
\text{FO}_{k}(S,J)
sentence
\phi
in signature
\sigma^{e}\setminus\{I\}
, there exists a
\text{MSO}_{k}
sentence
M(\phi)
in signature
\sigma
such that

A\vDash M(\phi)\Leftrightarrow\mathfrak{u}^{e}A\vDash\phi

Proof.

We can define
F
and
M
by recursion on
MSO
and
FO
formulas (respectively):

\displaystyle F(x_{i}=x_{j})

\displaystyle=v_{i}{:}\mathbf{f}=v_{j}{:}\mathbf{f}

\displaystyle F(x_{i}\in X_{j})

\displaystyle=e(v_{i}{:}\mathbf{f},w_{j}{:}\mathbf{m})

\displaystyle F(R(x_{i_{1}},\dots,x_{i_{r}}))

\displaystyle=R(v_{i_{1}}{:}\mathbf{f},\dots,v_{i_{r}}{:}\mathbf{f})

\displaystyle F(\varphi\land\psi)

\displaystyle=F(\varphi)\land F(\psi)

\displaystyle F(\neg\varphi)

\displaystyle=\neg F(\varphi)

\displaystyle F(\exists x_{i}\,\varphi)

\displaystyle=\exists\,v_{i}{:}\mathbf{f}\,F(\varphi)

\displaystyle F(\exists X_{i}\,\varphi)

\displaystyle=\exists\,w_{i}{:}\mathbf{m}\,F(\varphi)

\displaystyle M(v_{i}{:}\mathbf{f}=v_{j}{:}\mathbf{f})

\displaystyle=x_{i}=x_{j}

\displaystyle M(e(v_{i}{:}\mathbf{f},w_{j}{:}\mathbf{m}))

\displaystyle=x_{i}\in X_{j}

\displaystyle M(R(v_{i_{1}}{:}\mathbf{f},\dots,v_{i_{r}}{:}\mathbf{f}))

\displaystyle=R(x_{i_{1}},\dots,x_{i_{r}})

\displaystyle M(\varphi\land\psi)

\displaystyle=M(\varphi)\land M(\psi)

\displaystyle M(\neg\varphi)

\displaystyle=\neg M(\varphi)

\displaystyle M(\exists\,v_{i}{:}\mathbf{f}\,\varphi)

\displaystyle=\exists x_{i}\,M(\varphi)

\displaystyle M(\exists\,w_{i}{:}\mathbf{m}\,\varphi)

\displaystyle=\exists X_{i}\,M(\varphi)

The proofs that
A\vDash\phi\Leftrightarrow\mathfrak{u}^{e}A\vDash F(\phi)
and
A\vDash M(\phi)\Leftrightarrow\mathfrak{u}^{e}A\vDash\phi
are immediate from the standard semantics of
\vDash
.
∎

With this lemma and previous results about the many-sorted
\mathbb{E}_{k}
, we obtain the proof of
28

Proof of theorem
28
.

\displaystyle A\equiv_{\text{MSO}_{k}}B

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\mathfrak{u}^{e}A\equiv_{\text{FO}_{k}(S,J)}\mathfrak{u}^{e}B

by lemma
A.15

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\mathfrak{t}^{J}\mathfrak{u}^{e}A\leftrightarrow_{\mathbb{E}_{k}}\mathfrak{t}^{J}\mathfrak{u}^{e}B

by prop.
27

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\mathfrak{t}^{e}A\leftrightarrow_{\mathbb{E}_{k}}\mathfrak{t}^{e}B

\displaystyle\mathfrak{t}^{e}=\mathfrak{t}^{J}\circ\mathfrak{u}^{e}

Moreover, since the condition in the second item of proposition
27
is satisfied for our choice of
J
and
\mathbf{cs}(\sigma,\mathbf{f})
, we can obtain
A\equiv_{\text{MSO}_{k}}B\Leftrightarrow\mathfrak{t}^{e}_{\text{nc}}A\leftrightarrow_{\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}}\mathfrak{t}^{e}_{\text{nc}}B
using the same argument.
∎

A.11
Proofs for section
4.3

See

Proof.

This proof proceeds similarly to the proof that
{\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R}}
is FO-
\mathbb{E}_{k}
-smooth.
Axioms
(F1)
and
(F2)
follow automatically because the underlying function of
\kappa
is the two-sorted identity function.

To check that
\kappa
is a smooth Kleisli law, we to check that
\kappa
satisfies the smoothness axioms from
(S1)
and
(S2’)
from lemma
A.8
.

For
(S1)
, we can repeat the verification of
(S1)
as in lemma
21
nearly verbatim, but instead of using the relations in
R,P_{i_{z}}\in\sigma
, we use the corresponding relations
R,P_{i_{z}}\in\sigma^{e}
. By definition, the interpretations of these relations are the same.

For
(S2’)
, suppose
\pi\colon P\to\mathbb{E}_{k}P
is a path
r_{1}\sqsubset_{\pi}r_{2}\sqsubset_{\pi}\dots\sqsubset_{\pi}r_{n}
and
f\colon\pi\to[\alpha]
is a multilinear map. The proof that
\widehat{f}
has a minimal decomposition follows nearly verbatim to the
FO
case in lemma
21
. The key difference is that
\kappa
is the two-sorted identity function.

In addition to demonstrating that
\kappa
is a Kleisli law. We must also show there exists a natural transformation
\theta:{\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R,e}}\circ\mathfrak{t}^{e}\rightarrow\mathfrak{t}^{e}\circ{\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R}}
such that
F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(\theta_{A})
is an open pathwise embedding. The components
\theta_{A}
are given by
S
-sorted functions where
\theta_{A,t}
sends an element
x\in A_{t}\in{\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R,e}}(\mathfrak{t}^{e}(A))
the same element in
\mathfrak{t}^{e}{\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R}}(A)
. The interpretation of
Q\in\sigma^{e}\setminus\{e,I\}
has the same tuples as the interpretation of
Q\in\sigma
(including
R
). Moreover, since
\mathfrak{t}^{e}{\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R}}(A)
and
{\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R,e}}\mathfrak{t}^{e}(A)
have the same universe, the membership relation (consequently, interpretations of
e
) and the identity relation (consequently, interpretations of
I
) are the same, so we have that
\theta_{A}
is a
\sigma^{e}
-morphism. Since
\theta_{A}
is the
S
-sorted identity function, the underlying set function of
F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(\theta_{A})
is the identity and it is easy to check that
F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(\theta_{A})
is an open pathwise embedding.
∎

A.12
Proofs for section
4.4

We first verify the following claim.

Lemma A.16
.

\theta\colon\mathfrak{t}^{e}(A)\bowtie\mathfrak{t}^{e}(B)\to\mathfrak{t}^{e}(A\mathbin{\parallel}B)
is a strong onto homomorphism.

Proof.

Since
\theta
is defined as the identity function on the
\mathbf{f}
-sort, this holds true for the
\sigma_{3}
-reduct and the
\mathbf{f}
-sort part of
\theta
. On the
\mathbf{m}
-sort,
\theta
is clearly onto. So we only need to check that the
e
relation is reflected. Let
a\in A
,
M\subseteq A
and
N\subseteq B
. Assume that
(\mathfrak{i}(a),\mathfrak{i}(M)\cup\mathfrak{i}(N))\in e^{\mathfrak{t}^{e}(A\mathbin{\parallel}B)}
, i.e.
\mathfrak{i}(a)\in\mathfrak{i}(M)\cup\mathfrak{i}(N)
. If
a
is non-constant then clearly
a\in M
and so
(a,(M,N))\in e^{\mathfrak{t}^{e}(A)\bowtie\mathfrak{t}^{e}(B)}
. Otherwise,
a
is a constant, i.e.
a=c^{A}
for some
c\in\mathbf{cs}(\sigma)
. Then, no matter if
a\in\mathfrak{i}(M)
or
a\in\mathfrak{i}(N)
, we have that
(c^{A\mathbin{\parallel}B},(M,N))\in e^{\mathfrak{t}^{e}(A)\bowtie\mathfrak{t}^{e}(B)}
, which concludes the proof because
\iota(a)=\iota(c^{A})=c^{A\mathbin{\parallel}B}
.
∎

The fact that
F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}}(\theta)
is component-wise an open pathwise embedding then follows from the following.

Lemma A.17
.

For a multi-sorted signature
\sigma
, if
q
is a strong onto homomorphism in
\mathcal{R}^{S}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma)
then
F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}}(q)
is an open pathwise-embedding.

Proof.

In the following, we will write
U\dashv F
for the adjunction
U^{\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}}\dashv F^{\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}}
. Observe that the correspondence

\displaystyle\hom(U(\pi),A)\cong\hom(\pi,F(A)),

(10)

for
\pi
an
\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}
-path and
A
a (multi-sorted)
\sigma
-structure, restrict to a bijection between strong homomorphisms
U(\pi)\to A
and (path) embeddings
\pi\to F(A)
.

To check that
F(q)\colon F(A)\to F(B)
is a pathwise-embedding, let
e\colon\pi\rightarrowtail F(A)
be a path embedding. By the above, this embedding corresponds to a strong homomorphism
e^{r}\colon U(\pi)\rightarrowtail A
. Then,
q\circ e^{r}
is a strong homomorphism
e^{r}\colon U(\pi)\rightarrowtail B
because
q
is also strong. Therefore, the corresponding coalgebra morphism
(q\circ e^{r})^{l}\colon\pi\rightarrowtail F(B)
is an embedding and
F(q)\circ e=(q\circ e^{r})^{l}
by naturality of (
10
).

For openness, let
\pi
and
\rho
be paths and the following is a commutative square of path embeddings shown on left below.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(q)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U(\pi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U(\rho)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U(h)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

In view of (
10
), we have a commuting diagram on the right above, giving us that
\pi
corresponds to a word (of non-constants)
[x_{1}:s_{1},\dots,x_{n}:s_{n}]
in
A
and
\rho
corresponds to a word (of non-constants)
[y_{1}:s^{\prime}_{1},\dots,y_{m}:s^{\prime}_{m}]
where
n\leq m
,
s_{i}=s^{\prime}_{i}
and
q(x_{i})=y_{i}
, for
1\leq i\leq n
. Since
q
is onto, there exist
x_{j}\in A_{s_{j}}
such that
q_{s_{j}}(x_{j})=y_{j}
, for
n+1\leq j\leq m
. We define
d\colon U(\rho)\to A
by sending
y_{i}\mapsto x_{i}
(for
1\leq i\leq m
). It is easy to see that
d
is a homomorphism because
q
is a strong homomorphism. Also, the map
d
makes both triangles in the diagram commute, by definition. Consequently, we have a coalgebra homomorphism
d^{r}\colon\rho\to F(A)
making the obvious triangles on the left diagram above commute.
∎

Lemma A.18
.

\kappa\colon\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}(A\bowtie B)\to\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}(A)\bowtie\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}(B)
is a smooth Kleisli law.

Proof.

The proof of the fact that
\kappa
is a well-defined natural transformation that satisfies
(F1)
,
(F2)
,
(S1)
, and
(S2’)
is identical to the single-sorted proof of the same, in the proof lemma
22
above, with the only addition that we have to keep track of sorts.

Also,
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}})
has equalisers of reflexive pairs. This follows from lemma
14
, adapted verbatim to the multi-sorted setting. Since
\kappa
is a Kleisli law, the functor
\bowtie
lifts to a functor
\mathbin{\widehat{\bowtie}}\colon\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}})\times\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}})\to\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}})
and so
\kappa
is smooth by theorem
20
and lemma
A.8
.
∎

This now follows from the observations above.
See
31

A.13
Proofs for section
5

See
33

Proof.

For the proof we build a (bottom-up finite) tree automaton. The set of states of this automaton is the set
S=\{1,\,\dots,\,(t_{1}+\dots+t_{n})\}
where
t_{i}
is the total number of
\leftrightarrow_{\mathbb{C}_{i}}^{\mathfrak{t}_{i}}
-equivalence classes in
\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma_{i})
, for
i=1,\dots,n
. Since every comonad
\mathbb{C}_{i}
has finite MSO type and since we only have finitely many categories, we know that
t_{1}+\dots+t_{n}
is a finite number. For the alphabet we take the set
\{o_{H}\mid H\in\mathcal{H}\}
of names for operations in
\mathcal{H}
, where the arity of
o_{H}
is precisely the arity of the functor
H
(ignoring the types).

Next, let
l_{A}\in S
be the label corresponding to the
\leftrightarrow_{\mathbb{C}_{i}}^{\mathfrak{t}_{i}}
-equivalence class of
A
, for every
A\in\mathcal{R}(\sigma_{i})
and
1\leq i\leq n
. Then, for transition rules, given
H\in\mathcal{H}
of type
\mathcal{R}(\sigma_{i(1)})\times\dots\times\mathcal{R}(\sigma_{i(u)})\to\mathcal{R}(\sigma_{i(u+1)})
and structures
A_{l}\in\sigma_{i(l)}
, for
1\leq l\leq u
, we add the transition

o_{H}(l_{A_{1}},\dots,l_{A_{n}})\kern 5.0pt\leadsto\kern 5.0ptl_{H(A_{1},\dots,A_{n})}.

We see that this transition is deterministic because every
H\in\mathcal{H}
is MSO-
(\mathbb{C}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{C}_{n})
-smooth.

Lastly, we define the accepting states as those
l_{A}\in S
such that the
\tau
-reduct of
A
is in
\Delta
. This, is well-defined by assumption (3).

Now, given any term
t_{A}
, we build a tree
T_{A}
by translating every constant
B\in\mathcal{B}
into the label
l_{B}
and every operation application of
H\in\mathcal{H}
into the letter
o_{H}
. Recall that checking if an input tree is accepted can be done in linear time, for any tree automaton. Lastly, observe that, by construction,
T_{A}
is accepted if and only if
A\in\Delta
.
∎

A.14
Proofs for section
5.1

Recall that
\operatorname{fg}_{C,D}\colon\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma_{C})\to\mathcal{R}_{0}(\sigma_{D})
, for
D\subseteq C\subseteq\{c_{1},\dots,c_{m}\}
,
is the operation which sends a relational structure
A
, in signature
\sigma_{C}
, to its
\sigma_{D}
-reduct. This operation naturally extends to the two-sorted functor
\operatorname{fg}_{C,D}^{e}\colon\mathcal{R}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma^{e}_{C})\to\mathcal{R}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma^{e}_{D})
, which acts as the identity mapping on morphisms.

We immediately see that
\mathfrak{t}^{e}\circ\operatorname{fg}_{C,D}\cong\operatorname{fg}_{C,D}^{e}\circ\mathfrak{t}^{e}
. Therefore, for
MSO
-
\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}
-smoothness of
\operatorname{fg}_{C,D}
, we only need to find a smooth Kleisli law

\kappa\colon\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}\circ\operatorname{fg}_{C,D}^{e}\to\operatorname{fg}_{C,D}^{e}\circ\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}.

Given
A
in
\mathcal{R}_{\text{nc}}(\sigma^{e}_{C})
, we divide its universe as
A_{\mathbf{f}}\uplus A_{\mathbf{m}}=A_{0}\uplus C_{0}\uplus D
where
D=\{c^{A}\mid c\in\mathbf{cs}(\sigma_{D})\}
,
C_{0}=\{c^{A}\mid c\in\mathbf{cs}(\sigma_{C})\setminus\mathbf{cs}(\sigma_{D})\}
and
A_{0}=A_{\mathbf{f}}\uplus A_{\mathbf{m}}\setminus(D\cup C_{0})
.
Recall that the universe of
\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}(\operatorname{fg}_{C,D}^{e}(A))
consists of
(A_{0}\cup C_{0})^{\leq k}\uplus\mathbf{cs}(\sigma_{D})
whereas the universe of
\operatorname{fg}_{C,D}^{e}(\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}(A))
consists of
A_{0}^{\leq k}\uplus\mathbf{cs}(\sigma_{C})
, with only
\mathbf{cs}(\sigma_{D})\subseteq\mathbf{cs}(\sigma_{C})
marked as constants.
Define
\kappa
componentwise on non-constants by (while preserving the sorts as usual):

\displaystyle\kappa\colon\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}(\operatorname{fg}_{C,D}^{e}(A))

\displaystyle\kern 5.0pt\longrightarrow\kern 5.0pt\operatorname{fg}_{C,D}^{e}(\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}(A)),

\displaystyle w\in(A_{0}\cup C_{0})^{\leq k}

\displaystyle\kern 5.0pt\longmapsto\kern 5.0pt\begin{cases}\mathtt{ex}(w)\in A_{0}^{\leq k}&\text{ if }\varepsilon(w)\in A_{0}\\[5.0pt]
c^{\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}(A)}&\text{ if }\varepsilon(w)=c^{A}\in C_{0}\end{cases}

Here
\mathtt{ex}([x_{1}:s_{1},\dots,x_{n}:s_{n}])
is defined as
[x_{i}:s_{i}\mid x_{i}\in A_{0}]
. It is clear that
\kappa
is a
\sigma_{D}
-structure homomorphism. Furthermore, it sends non-constants to non-constants since
c^{\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}(A)}
with
c^{A}\in C_{0}
is no longer a constant in
\operatorname{fg}_{C,D}^{e}(\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}(A))
. The reasoning justifying that axioms
(F1)
and
(F2)
hold is exactly the same for lemma
22
and
(S1)
holds for trivial reasons. Lastly, we check
(S2’)
. Assume we are given a path embedding, in terms of a multilinear map
e
as shown below.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{fg}_{C,D}^{e}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}(P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}\operatorname{fg}_{C,D}^{e}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{fg}_{C,D}^{e}(\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{fg}_{C,D}^{e}(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}(e)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}

Let
x_{1}:s_{1}\sqsubset_{\pi}x_{1}:s_{1}\sqsubset_{\pi}\dots\sqsubset_{\pi}x_{n}:s_{n}
be the chain of non-constant elements of
P
. The commutativity of the above square means that, for every
i
,

\kappa([a_{1}:s_{1},\dots,a_{i}:s_{i}])=\alpha(a_{i})

where
a_{j}
is set to be
e(x_{j})
, for
1\leq j\leq n
. In other words, if
a_{i}\in A_{0}
it must be that the word
\kappa([a_{1}:s_{1},\dots,a_{i}:s_{i}])
is the chain of non-constant elements
\sqsubset_{\alpha}
-below
a_{i}
in
A
. If
a_{i}\in C_{0}
then we automatically get that
\kappa([a_{1}:s_{1},\dots,a_{i}:s_{i}])=a_{i}
and also
\alpha(a_{i})=a_{i}
, making this automatically satisfied. Therefore, the word
[a_{1}:s_{1},\dots,a_{n}:s_{n}]
consists of elements from a path
(P^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
in
(A,\alpha)
interleaved by constant elements from
C_{0}
. It is immediate that
e
factors through
\operatorname{fg}_{C,D}^{e}(e^{\prime})
of the embedding
e^{\prime}\colon(P^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})\rightarrowtail(A,\alpha)
and that this
(P^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
is minimal possible. As a result we obtain the following by theorem
20
and lemma
A.8
.

Lemma A.19
.

For every
D\subseteq C\subseteq\{c_{1},\dots,c_{m}\}
, the operation
\operatorname{fg}_{C,D}\colon\mathcal{R}(\sigma_{C})\to\mathcal{R}(\sigma_{D})
is
MSO
-
\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nc}}
-smooth.

A.15
Proofs for section
5.2

What remains to check to support theorem
37
is the following claim.

Lemma A.20
.

For every
i,j\in\{1,\dots,m\}
, the operation
\operatorname{relab}_{i,j}:\mathcal{R}(\sigma_{m})\to\mathcal{R}(\sigma_{m})
is
MSO
-
\mathbb{E}_{k}
-smooth.

Proof.

It is clear that
\operatorname{relab}_{i,j}\colon\mathcal{R}(\sigma_{m})\rightarrow\mathcal{R}(\sigma_{m})
and the corresponding two-sorted operation
\operatorname{relab}_{i,j}^{e}\colon\mathcal{R}^{S}(\sigma^{e}_{m})\rightarrow\mathcal{R}^{S}(\sigma^{e}_{m})
are functorial. We need to define smooth Kleisli law

\kappa\colon\mathbb{E}_{k}\circ\operatorname{relab}_{i,j}^{e}\rightarrow\operatorname{relab}_{i,j}^{e}\circ\mathbb{E}_{k}

The components
\kappa_{A}
are given by
S
-sorted functions where
\kappa_{A,t}
sends a word
w\in(\mathbb{E}_{k}(\operatorname{relab}_{i,j}(A)))_{t}
to the same word
w\in(\operatorname{relab}_{i,j}(\mathbb{E}_{k}(A)))_{t}
for
t\in\{\mathbf{f},\mathbf{m}\}=S
. This is the same definition, on the level of
S
-sorted sets, as the smooth Kleisli law used to witness that
{\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R}}
is
MSO
-
\mathbb{E}_{k}
-smooth. We also need to exhibit a natural transformation
\theta:\operatorname{relab}_{i,j}^{e}\circ\mathfrak{t}^{e}\rightarrow\mathfrak{t}^{e}\circ\operatorname{relab}_{i,j}
. As in the case of
{\operatorname{join}_{\bar{i}}^{R}}
, we define the components
\theta_{A}
to be the
S
-sorted identify function sending an element
a\in\operatorname{relab}_{i,j}^{e}(\mathfrak{t}^{e}(A))
to the same element
a\in\mathfrak{t}^{e}(\operatorname{relab}_{i,j}A)
. Consequently, the proof of lemma
30
can be adapted to demonstrate that
\operatorname{relab}_{i,j}
is
MSO
-
\mathbb{E}_{k}
-smooth.
∎
